### 2026-09-21 — REFRESH round on PR #791 across the review-owner boundary (#756), `review-response` step `Address findings`, `xezar-review-response`, claude (Sonnet 5) — real-task observed
- Input: the `## Integration SKIPPED` comment on PR #791 (a real `base.moved` refusal with a genuine content conflict in `packages/xezar/src/skills-remote.ts`, between main's `5df694d0` #771 restructure and this PR's #777/#793 additions), the PR body, and a dispatch brief naming the PR's own branch `xez/f8099f7e` while this task's own branch is `xez/770d8428` — the #756 split where a review-response task does not own the PR branch it must deliver to.
- Observed: **`worktree-git.sh commit` never entered the picture — `merge-recovery.sh` is the only guarded path for a conflicted merge, and it refuses retroactive authorization.** The first attempt ran `git merge origin/main` before recording intent; resolving the conflict and staging it left `worktree-preflight.sh` refusing with `gitstate.clean` (an in-progress `MERGE_HEAD`), and `merge-recovery.sh record-intent` itself refuses to record an intent while a git operation is already in flight ("a record written now would be derived from the very state it claims to authorize"). The only way through was `git merge --abort` (safe here — the resolution had just been made and its content was saved to `/tmp` first, so nothing was lost), `record-intent` against a clean tree, redo the identical `git merge`, restore the same resolved file, then `merge-recovery.sh check` and `commit`. Worth naming because the ordering requirement is invisible until the merge is already conflicted and cannot be discovered by reading `worktree-git.sh --help` alone — a REFRESH brief that says "git merge" should say to run `merge-recovery.sh record-intent` FIRST, matching the same lesson already on record from #330 (`docs/dogfooding.md` line 764).
- Observed: **the branch-ownership split (#756) is real and the guarded push handles it cleanly.** This task's own branch (`xez/770d8428`) started with zero commits over `main`, so resetting it to the PR's tip (`git reset --hard origin/xez/f8099f7e`) before merging cost nothing — a task with prior commits of its own could not take this shortcut. The resulting merge commit lived on both refs at once (this task's checked-out branch, and — after `worktree-git.sh push xez/f8099f7e` — the PR's own remote branch), verified by the delivery guard's four checks (recorded branch, recorded head equals `HEAD_SHA`, recorded base is an ancestor of head, and the live remote tip still equals the recorded base) before it allowed a non-force push.
- Observed: the conflict itself resolved cleanly with no functional ambiguity — main's and the PR's changes touched adjacent, non-overlapping additions in the same function bodies (a new type/function pair each, plus one line at the tail of `loadTeamSkills` where both sides' effects needed to run together). `git merge-tree` had already told the truth in the SKIPPED comment: exactly one file overlapped, and the fold was a single line (`teamSourceCountByRoot.set(...)` alongside `return { skills: out, sources }`).
- Regression/control: the PR's own six vitest suites (118 tests) and its 17-case `node:test` suite re-ran green at the merge commit, plus `npm run typecheck` (all four workspaces) clean — all re-run AFTER the merge commit was written, not before, so the evidence is tied to the pushed head rather than to the pre-merge resolution.
- Remaining limit: no canonical gate list was run in this step by design (the workflow's `gates` step is the run's one canonical attempt); CI status at the new head was not observed from this step.

### 2026-09-21 — MCP test fixtures pin HOME so the developer's skill catalog cannot leak (#671), `bug-fix` step `Reproduce, diagnose and fix`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, pi — real-task observed, fixture-tested, live-verified on the failing machine

- Input: the gate-return diagnosis for run `dd76265e` (PR #783) and the PR's author note. Both were correct and reproducible here: `npm test -- packages/xezar/src/mcp/tools/task-create.test.ts` was 2 failed | 115 passed (117) with the developer's real `~/.agents/skills/xez-issue-create/SKILL.md` present, and 117 passed under a throwaway `HOME`. The diagnosis alone would have left this failing on every local gate run; the brief's bounded fix is what closes it.
- Observed: the pin cannot live in a fixture's `setup()`. `skills.ts` resolves its two global skill dirs from `os.homedir()` ONCE, in the module-level `GLOBAL_SKILL_DIRS` const, so by the time a fixture assigns `process.env.HOME` the paths are already fixed. The fix is therefore a module-load side effect, and the shared testkit has to be a test file's FIRST import — an import-order contract, not a helper call. The diagnosis's "the fixture pins XEZ_HOME but not HOME" was accurate but incomplete about the mechanism; the module-load timing is what chose the shape.
- Observed: the brief asked for the pin in "the shared testkit the MCP tool tests already use". No such MCP tool fixture existed. The one shared testkit most MCP tool tests import, `operation-id.testkit.ts`, is imported after `server.ts` in every one of its 13 importers, so a module-body pin there would have run too late to be a guarantee. A dedicated `mcp-test-home.testkit.ts` was created and imported first in all 27 MCP test files that build a project workspace or read the skill catalog.
- Observed: `packages/xezar/src/state-path-scan.test.ts` deliberately skips `.test.ts`/`.testkit.ts` ("a fixture legitimately builds a fake home"), so a testkit that reads `process.env.HOME` is not a product-code finding and needed no allowlist entry.
- Red proof (scratch copy, never `git stash`): pin removed + a scratch HOME carrying `.agents/skills/xez-issue-create/SKILL.md` -> the two named cases fail at `task-create.test.ts:978` (`findIssueCreateSkill` returned the global skill record) and `:985` (`startBodies` carried `skill: xez-issue-create` instead of `workflow: quick-task`); the same tree with the pin -> 117 passed. The retained `mcp-test-home.test.ts` case is red without the pin at its own control assertion. Details, paths and sha256s are in the run's `BREAK_671_MCP_FIXTURE_HOME` record.
- Observed: `audit-redaction.test.ts` is a "data" file to grep and `file` because it carries NUL bytes as fixture content; that predates this change and the MCP suite is green over it.

### 2026-09-21 — The gate lease (#672 G1–G3), `feature-implementation` step `implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed
- Input: the accepted `677-spec.md` § "Wave 5 — #672, the gate lease" (slices G1+G2+G3, G4 explicitly out), the leader's decisions on G2 option (a), Q6 (a fixed machine-wide lock path) and Q7 (a) (a 20-minute source constant plus a load-bearing kit rule). Deliverable: `resources.gateSlots`, `packages/xezar/src/core/gate-lease.ts` + its suite, the `xezar lease gates` verb, `.xezar/checks/lib/gate-lease.sh`, ten new `infra-tests.sh` cases and the four documents the spec names.
- Observed: **a "do not touch `packages/web`" boundary cannot survive a required field in a shared contract.** Adding `gateSlots` to `workspaceConfigResponseSchema` broke `tsc` in nine cockpit TEST fixtures that build a whole response literal, so `npm run typecheck` was red until each gained `gateSlots: 1`. The boundary was aimed at the G4 control and that stayed unbuilt, but the instruction as written was not satisfiable: a contract field is a compile-time obligation on every construction site of the type, and `packages/web` holds nine of them. Worth stating in a brief as "no NEW UI" rather than "no web files".
- Observed: **`parseArgs` is strict before a subcommand ever sees its own flags.** `xezar lease gates --status-file X -- cmd` died on "Unknown option '--status-file'" because the global parse runs first, so the flag had to be registered globally with a written reason — the same arrangement `--instance` and `--single-project` already carry. The command tail still has to come from `process.argv`, because `parseArgs` folds everything after `--` into `positionals` and the boundary is the one thing the verb needs.
- Observed: **`tsx` cannot start under this project's own `TMPDIR`.** A task worktree's `TMPDIR` is `<primary>/.local/xezar/tmp/<runId>` — 89 characters — and `tsx` opens a unix socket under it for its own IPC, which caps at 104 bytes; it died with `EINVAL: listen` before reaching any repository code. Since the kit resolves the lease verb through `tsx` whenever there is no dist build, the lease would have silently failed open on every real gate run in this repository — fail-open hiding a total no-op. Both the kit helper and the fixtures now shorten `TMPDIR` for that one child.
- Observed (gate-return round 1): **one `resources` field ripples into four assertions an author's focused tests never reach.** The `gates` step came back red on `mcp-api-doc.test.ts` (two snapshots), `mcp-reference-route.test.ts` (which re-reads the generated `mcp-api.json`) and `project-config.test.ts` (which enumerates the `resources` keys) — every one a consequence of `gateSlots` existing, none of them a file this change edited. The author-side rule "run the focused tests for what you changed" does not find them, because the changed thing is a schema and the failing suites are generated-doc gates; a contract field added to `resources` should be followed by `npm test -- packages/xezar/src/mcp -u` in the same authoring step.
- Regression/control: the four breaks the spec names are proved red on scratch copies (see `RED_PROOFS` in this run's evidence dir, with the sha256 of each file before and after). The control that pins the feature's *point* rather than its mechanism is the infra case whose assertion is an ORDER — `a-start a-end b-start` — because a second run that did not serialise could not produce it whatever the timing.
- Observed (review response, round 1, run `535cca57`, `address-review-findings`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Opus 5): **a background job inherits the shell's descriptors, and that is how a "fail-open" lock became a lock nobody could reclaim.** The taking shell opened the FIFO read-write on fd 9 and started the lease verb with `&`, so the verb was itself a writer on the pipe it was waiting to read: a `repo-gates.sh` killed without its EXIT trap left a holder whose `read` never reached EOF, a verb that never exited, and a heartbeat that kept the slot fresh against both reclaim mechanisms at once. Neither pid liveness nor the stale bound could see it, because both of them were being told the truth — the process really was alive. The fix is one token (`9>&-` on the verb's command line); the lesson is that *the mechanism that reclaims a lock must not be able to be fed by the thing it reclaims*. Two more from the same round, both in code that read as safe: `exec 9>&- 2>/dev/null` makes stderr permanently `/dev/null` for the whole script, because `exec` with no command makes every redirection on the line permanent (the fail-open path then ran the gates with `GATES ABORTED` discarded); and a swallowed `rm` failure inside `takeOverIfStale` answered "taken over", which the caller reads as "retry at once" — so an unremovable object at a slot path turned a bounded wait into an unbounded spin at 162 % CPU. A `.catch(() => undefined)` on a step whose SUCCESS the caller branches on is the shape to grep for.
- Observed (same round): **a check that pipes into `grep -q` under `set -o pipefail` reports the opposite of what it asserts.** The new "the check never crashes" case was written as `node … | grep -qF TypeError`; pipefail hands back node's own non-zero exit, so the case printed `ok` in the very run where its sibling was red on the crash it was looking for. It was caught only because the deliberate break was run through the whole suite rather than through the one case. Capture the output, then search it.
- Remaining limit: the lease's own acquire/release path inside `repo-gates.sh` is proved by sourcing `lib/gate-lease.sh` directly, never by a full canonical gate run, because an author may not run that list; whether the lease behaves under several REAL concurrent gate runs on a loaded machine is unobserved and is what the `gates` step of this run will be the first evidence of.

### 2026-09-21 — The gate-slot control (#672 G4), `feature-implementation` step `implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed
- Input: the leader's accepted G4 brief — a Settings → Resources control for `resources.gateSlots` over the existing `PUT /api/v1/workspace/config` route, with "no new route, no new schema", an empty field clearing the key back to the derived default, and one parity test proving both doors accept the same shape. Deliverable: the control, the contract's `null` spelling behind it, the route's delete branch, eight tests across three suites and four documents.
- Observed: **"no new schema" and "clearing deletes the key" were not both satisfiable as written, and the brief could not know it.** G1 had shipped `gateSlots` deliberately non-nullable, with a comment saying so ("No `null`: there is no 'unlimited' spelling"), and the route had no delete branch — so an emptied field had nothing to send. The resolution was to widen the existing field rather than add a shape (`.nullable()`, plus the `=== null → delete` branch the sibling `agentDefaults.runner` already had), because the `null` G1 refused was an *unlimited* spelling and the `null` G4 needs is a *clear*. Those are different values wearing the same token, and the only reason the reversal is safe is that #672 is unreleased. A brief that says "the key is already there" is worth checking against the key's own comments before trusting it.
- Observed: **a test written by the previous slice of the same issue is the thing a later slice has to edit, and it reads exactly like an out-of-scope test.** `workspace-api.test.ts` listed `{ gateSlots: null }` among the 400s, with a comment explaining why. Under the standing "never repair a test outside this pull request's scope" rule that looks untouchable; it is in fact the most in-scope test there is — same key, same issue, behaviour this change deliberately reverses. The distinction that matters is not "did my diff create this file" but "is this test asserting the behaviour I was asked to change".
- Observed: **a response schema that reports only the EFFECTIVE value costs the control its cleared state.** `workspaceConfigResponseSchema.resources.gateSlots` is a plain `z.number()` on purpose (absent and an explicit 1 behave identically), so after a clear the pane re-reads `1` and cannot render "nothing stored" the way the memory field renders `null`. Harmless here — the two states really are the same behaviour — but it is the reason the field keeps local state and the hint names the default in words instead of the control showing it. A future key where absent and the default differ could not reuse this shape.
- Observed: **one `.nullable()` on a contract field re-rendered a generated document.** `mcp-api-doc.test.ts` failed on two snapshots for a row that changed from `integer` to `integer or null`; `npm test -- packages/xezar/src/mcp/mcp-api-doc.test.ts -u` is the documented fix and belongs in the authoring step for any contract edit, not just an added key. This is the second slice of #672 to meet it (G1–G3 recorded the same thing about an added field).
- Regression/control: both AC proofs were run against scratch copies of the three pre-fix sources (paths and sha256 in this run's evidence dir, `red-proof.txt`). Red without the fix: the route's `null`-clears case (400, not 200), all three cockpit cases (no control in the DOM) and the MCP parity case (`expected number, received null`). **Honest split:** the route-level "out of range is refused" assertion is a green-either-way GUARD, not a red proof — the 1–16 bound shipped with G1 and this change does not touch it; the out-of-range proof that is genuinely red is the cockpit one, whose control did not exist.
- Remaining limit: "takes effect without a restart" is verified by reading the mechanism (`xezar lease gates` calls `loadWorkspaceConfig()` per invocation, in a process the gate script spawns) and by the semaphore assertions, not by watching a real second gate run pick up a changed count live. The design review the `needs-design` label asks for is also outstanding: this run's own checks say the control works, not that a number input is the right shape for a limit whose only safe value is the default.

### 2026-09-21 — the gate lease's fail-open branches, and a merge that broke a file it never touched (#672 PR #805, review round 2), `address-review-findings`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed, scratch-copy proven

- Input: the Fable `## Code re-check` at `ee61bd0b` — every round-1 finding RESOLVED, REQUEST CHANGES for one carried-over Major (the branch `CONFLICTING` against `main` `ef583f4f`, so GitHub built no merge ref and the required CI had never run on any head of the PR), one new Minor (three fail-open branches with no test) and two comment Nits; plus the leader's disposition that Minor 8 stays an accepted documented limit. The re-check named its experiment before opening the diff — "enumerate every exit from the WAITING state" — and that table is what found the untested branches.
- Observed, and the one that cost a repair round: a CLEAN textual merge is not a finished merge. `merge-recovery.sh` reported exactly one conflicted file, `BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md` § 9's `config.json` bullet (#798 had edited the same bullet), the resolution kept both sides' facts, the tree was clean — and `npm run typecheck` then failed. #798 also brought a NEW file whose resources fixture predates this branch making `resources.gateSlots` required, so two changes contradicted each other in a file neither of them touched and no marker could show it. The only thing that catches that is running the author's own checks AFTER the merge rather than reading the absence of markers as success; a round whose brief is "merge and re-gate" should expect to spend a repair round on exactly this.
- Observed: a fail-open branch that no test drives is indistinguishable from one that is not there. Its whole job is to be invisible when it works, which makes it the code least likely to be noticed when it stops working — and the reviewer's own measurement said so: delete any of the three branches and a 753-case suite stayed green. Each was cheap to pin once the TRIGGER was found, and finding the trigger was the whole task: a read-only attempt directory for "the FIFO cannot be created", a fake `tsx` that exits 1 for "the helper produced no status", and a fake verb that resolves its lease and then ignores both the release FIFO and SIGTERM for the bounded drop.
- Observed: `exec 9<>` had no natural trigger at all, and that is why it is its own branch. A read-only home produces the SIBLING branch (`mkfifo` fails first), so the two cannot be told apart that way. A process descriptor limit below 9 separates them exactly — `mkfifo` still succeeds and only the open fails. The same limit makes bash print `redirection error` lines of its own, because bash juggles its internal descriptors above 10; that noise is the fixture's, is named in the case's comment, and the assertions are on the lease's own line.
- Regression/control: four red proofs on scratch copies of `lib/gate-lease.sh`, one deleted branch each, never `git stash` (its stack is shared with every worktree on this machine). Red and green run through ONE driver, so they are the same assertions rather than two descriptions of one. The unbounded variant of the drop never returned — which is the defect that case pins — so the proof needed a bound and a saved pid of its own to end it, never a command-line pattern. Driver, mutants, sha256s and the full transcript are in the run's evidence directory.
- Observed: a suite count that goes DOWN after you add cases is a fact to check, not a failure to report. 751 against the reviewer's 753 plus four new ones looked like a loss; one `git diff` showed it was #803 on `main` retiring six assertions of its own in the same file, and the merged tree still carries all 26 `gateSlots` mentions of this PR's own cases. Reporting the number without that check would have put a false alarm in the response comment.
- Remaining limit: every live trial here is macOS, as the re-check's were. The Linux behaviour of cases that start a real verb, SIGKILL a shell, lower a descriptor limit and read FIFOs is unknown until the `pull_request` CI run this round exists to unblock actually reports — which is why the carried-over Major was about a merge ref and not about code.

- Input: the release brief (`bump: minor`, `version: 0.17.0`) and `main` at `0d31ff81`; 110 first-parent

  26 are issue numbers and only one is a PR number of that release's window. Folding the fragments
  verbatim — which the skill requires — therefore leaves most kept PR numbers absent from the section.
  The check was satisfied as coverage instead: every one of the 109 kept PRs maps to a folded fragment
  (92), to one of the 9 direct `# Unreleased` bullets, or to a bullet written here.
- Observed: **`git describe --tags --abbrev=0 origin/main` names `v0.16.0` at `c662f93d`, the CHANGELOG
  commit, not the release bump `a8b25a77`** — so the tag range legitimately contains the
  `chore(release): v0.16.0 (#643)` manifest bump, which step 2 excludes.
- Observed: **one bullet for a PR merged after the tag sat inside the released `0.16.0` section.** PR
  #634's branch was cut from a `main` on which the `0.16.0` section was the top of the file and
  `# Unreleased` did not exist yet, so its changelog edit landed at the matching `## 🐛 Fixes` context
  inside `0.16.0`; `git diff c662f93d:CHANGELOG.md origin/main:CHANGELOG.md` shows it as the only change
  to the dated region. It was moved, verbatim, into the `0.17.0` section rather than duplicated.
- Regression/control: the skill's exclusion rule "a PR whose number already appears in a dated section"
  produced 20 false positives — 3-digit PR numbers of this release (`#803`, `#801`, `#797`, `#791`, …)
  collide with pre-rename issue numbers cited in the `0.10.0`/`0.9.2` sections. Excluding them would have
  dropped 20 PRs silently; the range check (`mergeCommit` is a first-parent commit of `v0.16.0..origin/main`)
  is the sound form of that rule and kept all 109.
- Remaining limit: the skill's `git diff --stat` list does not include the five `unreleased — ships in
  0.17.0` marks, but the release brief names flipping them as a release-candidate point and no other step
  in the workflow may write, so they are flipped in this commit (two lines in `BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md`,
  one in `docs/guide/11-configuration-reference.md`, one wrapped pair in
  `docs/guide/07-github-and-automations.md`). The pre-check's other two doc actions — an
  `## Upgrading to 0.17.0` README section and the 18 guide footers — are NOT named in the brief and the
  footer value is an explicitly unresolved owner decision, so neither was guessed here; both are reported

### 2026-09-21 — PR 4 of #467: linking out, and the two questions a predicate must not merge

- Input: the accepted spec's `### PR 4 — the cockpit: link out` plus § 3–4 and the appendix, read
  read-only from run `a0cc9e46`'s evidence directory, with PRs 1–3 already on the base. Deliverable:
  the third project-mode predicate, the sidebar's Other projects band, the palette's link-out rows,
  the global Tasks note, three red proofs and the design-system entries.
- The brief named the temptation before I met it: fold `instanceMode === 'project'` into
  `projectsLocked`. All three predicates read like "this cockpit is narrower than the default", and
  the merge compiles, types and looks tidy. What it actually does is delete the Add project control
  and the whole Projects group — `BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md` calls that a break. The red proof took
  four minutes and is the only thing that makes the refusal auditable rather than a matter of taste.
- The byte-identity pin for the untouched default path had no precedent here: `packages/web` carried
  no snapshot test at all before this PR. A committed `.snap` of the sidebar's `outerHTML` is what
  makes "one class moved" fail; the break proof moved `px-2.5` to `px-2` on the flat nav and the
  snapshot caught it. The cost is honest — a legitimate sidebar change now needs `-u` — and that is
  the point of the criterion.
- Two test-harness defects of my own, both found in self-review and both the same shape:
  `expect(optionalChained).not.toBeNull()` passes for `undefined`, so a `waitFor` resolved on the
  first tick with nothing rendered and the assertion after it read a value that was never there. A
  `?? null` fixes it. Worth knowing because the test still went green on the fix under test — it was
  green for the wrong reason.
- `window.location.assign` cannot be spied on in jsdom (`Cannot assign to read only property`), and
  `Object.create(window.location)` does not help. `vi.stubGlobal('location', { href, assign })` with
  a plain object does, because `window === globalThis` there.
- Browser spec deliberately not written: the suite shares one instance and one set of on-disk
  fixtures, and this feature needs a server booted in a different instance mode, which no shared
  boot can provide without changing what every other spec sees. Recorded in CAPABILITY and in the
  PR body rather than left as a silent gap.
- The gate return that was not mine: the product's own default-on skills update wrote this machine's
  `~/.agents/skills` catalog four minutes before the gate run, and two MCP tests that assert "this
  project has no skills" went red — `skills.ts` reaches `homedir()/.agents/skills` by design, and
  `vitest.setup.ts` pins `XEZ_HOME` but not `HOME`. Proof without editing anything: the same command
  under a throwaway `HOME` passes 117 of 117. CI never sees it (a runner has no such catalog), so it
  is a local-gate-only, machine-deterministic failure — the honest move on a gate return is a causal
  experiment, not a guess at "flake", and the second failure (a port TOCTOU race in
  `serve-port-memory`) really was one and passed on a clean re-run. Two red gates, two different
  answers, and the difference was one `HOME=` in front of the same command.
- Carried onto a fresh branch as a superseding candidate (run `ba557149`, PR supersedes #783) after
  the original branch spent both gate returns on out-of-scope failures that were later fixed on
  `main` (#785, #786). The mechanic that made the carry honest rather than a reset: the counter
  history was declared with `phase-record.sh counters init --predecessor <original runId>`, so the
  spent `gate-return` budget travels with the content instead of being laundered by the new branch.
  Readiness still passes, because "2 of 2 used" is at the limit and not over it — the new candidate
  gets its one gate run and no repair round. That is the right shape: a new branch may re-prove the
  same tree once, but it must not buy a fresh allowance to change it.

### 2026-09-21 — record the 2026-09-21 leader lessons 12–16, `docs-maintenance` step `Update docs`, `xezar-docs-maintenance`, claude (Opus 5) — real-task observed

- Input: the owner's standing rule of 2026-09-19 10:2x (every rule lives in committed project documentation, never in memory), the five leader lessons 12–16 and two recorded facts listed in the brief, and PR #795 (`871f4292`) as the shape and placement to follow. Deliverable: `.xezar/docs/model-routing.md` (two new § 6 rows, a new § 13 `### 2026-09-21 (continued)` entry), `.xezar/docs/leader-guide.md` (the leader-behaviour lessons and two checklist lines), this fragment, one `changelog.d/` fragment and the phase record.
- Observed: **a lesson list handed to a second docs task can already be recorded by the first, and "add a row" then silently means "add a duplicate".** Lesson 12 – a superseding run declares `counters init --none`, never `--predecessor` – was already a § 6 row and already a § 13 bullet, both landed by PR #795 four hours earlier with the same run id `98cc751d` as its evidence. The brief listed it as work to do. Writing the row again would have put the same rule in the table twice, which is exactly the failure the "each rule exactly once, in the section that owns it" discipline of #795 exists to prevent. The check that caught it was reading the current § 6 table before writing, not reading the brief: a lesson batch numbered by the leader does not carry which of its members are already committed, so the executor has to diff the list against the document rather than trust the numbering.
- Observed: **the § 13 record still needs the already-recorded lesson, even when § 6 does not.** The findings log is a dated record of what was true then, so an entry dated 2026-09-21 that silently omits lesson 12 would read as though the lesson was never raised that day. The resolution was to record it in § 13 as a repeat that points at the entry above and states that nothing was added to § 6 for it. A rule table and a findings log have different duplicate rules: the table refuses a second copy, the log requires the day's facts to be complete.
- Observed: **two of the five lessons are half-recorded already, and only the new half may be written.** Lesson 15 (`gh pr checks` can show no checks) extends a leader bullet from 2026-09-16 that already names `gh workflow run ci.yml --ref <branch>`; what is new is "no checks AT ALL, not merely pending" and "never a close/reopen". Lesson 13 sits beside the 2026-09-21 `REFRESH` row it does not replace. In both cases the edit is an extension of the existing sentence with its own dated attribution, not a new bullet, so the document keeps one home per rule while the dates stay honest about when each half was learned.
- Regression/control: docs-only, so there is no code regression to prove. The mechanical controls are the checks the brief names – `node .xezar/checks/catalog-check.mjs`, `bash .xezar/checks/changelog-check.sh` and `npm run typecheck` – plus `node scripts/check-links.mjs` and `node .xezar/checks/dogfooding-fragments.mjs --check .xezar/docs/dogfooding.d` for the two fragment shapes; the canonical gate list is the workflow's own `gates` step against this head, never an author run.
- Remaining limit: the evidence for every lesson is the leader's own dated statement in the brief, recorded here as stated and not re-derived from the run store. Runs `98cc751d`, `e842b53c`, `c03361ad` and `9395bcfb`, PR #782 (`2f195b78`) and issue #804 are cited as the brief gives them; this task read neither the run store nor those pull requests, and the § 13 entry says so by keeping every bullet to facts and run ids.
- Observed (round 1 of 2, review response): **the two Majors were the same defect seen twice – a dated record that reads as a live instruction – and the fix was to move the tense and the incident, not the content.** § 13's job is what happened and the leader guide's is what to do now, yet two § 13 bullets were headed as present-tense rules ("a `continue` note … re-answers a settled question", "`gh pr checks` can show no checks at all"), and one carried the leader's *diagnosis* ("under load") in place of the observed fact. The rewrite kept every run id, added the incidents the review supplied from the campaign timeline (`770d8428` at 04:43, the 06:24 dispatch on `xez/9395bcfb`, PR #807's port-65535 root cause) and ended each bullet with a pointer to the section that owns the rule; the Minor was two clocks in one dated entry (`e842b53c` stamped 03:09 UTC, `c03361ad` written "05:4x" against a CEST timeline whose failure minute is 05:52). Two things this round learned that a reader cannot get from one section: a findings log written by the same author in the same sitting as the rule it produced drifts into instruction voice, and the "each rule exactly once" discipline is only checkable by reading § 6, § 13 and the guide together – a § 13 bullet that restates the rule it points at looks harmless in isolation and is the third copy. Limits: the load now recorded (12, at the failure minute) is the timeline's 05:52 reading, not a re-measurement, and `c03361ad`'s own gate-return record says "under load ~15"; PR #807's root cause is quoted from the timeline's 06:12 line, and this round read neither that PR's diff nor the run store.

Reading the target package's own rule before copying the neighbour's diff is what kept the change legal.
- Observed: **`role="status"` alone gives a region an EMPTY accessible name, so `waitForRoleGone('status', …)` returns on its first attempt and reads as a wait without being one.** agent-browser says so out loud (`2 elements have role "status", but none match name … Names seen: ""`), but only to a spec that fails; a vacuous wait in a passing spec is silent. The first candidate here was exactly that and went red immediately, which is the only reason it was caught before commit — and `guide-13-mcp-leader-control.e2e.ts:105` has the same shape against an unlabelled region today (reported as a follow-up, not touched, because the brief forbids editing another test).

Six spec runs and five web builds took under four minutes in total. Probing the live shared cockpit (port 4323) with the CLI directly was the one dead end: that instance sat at its own section-level loading line, so the spec's own fixture server was the faster oracle.

Controls that pass both ways and are labelled so: the pre-edit spec against the clean build passes (the old mechanism is timing-dependent, not always-red), 26 files / 433 focused unit tests pass, `npm run typecheck` clean. Green in one vitest invocation with a second e2e file, twice (`settings-resources`, `settings-agents`).
- Remaining limit: requirement (3) of the brief needed no edit — `KNOWN_LOAD_FLAKES` carries `"MCP per-file coverage"` only and the leader guide already says no browser spec is on a rerun list — so it is reported rather than silently skipped. The whole suite was not run; the evidence is the two-file invocation the brief asked for, so a whole-suite ordering effect is unmeasured here.

### 2026-09-21 — `cli.instance` editable from Settings and the MCP (#467 PR 5), `feature-implementation` step `Implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed, scratch-copy proven

- Input: the accepted #467 spec sections 4.1–4.5 and 5 (PR 5), the owner's 2026-09-20 16:38 decision that all five PRs ship in 0.17.0, and four acceptance criteria that each had to be provable false. PRs 1–3 were on `main`; PR 4 (#790) was not merged, and the brief asked for independence from it, which held — nothing in this PR reaches into the switcher.
- Observed: a contract-first request schema made the MCP write surface free. `set_workspace_config` re-declares `setWorkspaceConfigInputSchema`, so adding `cli` to the contract opened the leader's door with no tool change at all; the only MCP work was the answer builder and two descriptions. The cost landed elsewhere and was not obvious from the brief: a REQUIRED new key on the response shape broke seven cockpit test fixtures, and a new cockpit Settings section touched four list-shaped tests (two section inventories, a file-note map, a loading-rhythm map) plus a closed MCP inventory whose record count is asserted in two suites and republished in a generated coverage map. The feature is four files; keeping the repository's own guards true is eleven more.
- Regression/control: on a scratch copy of the worktree (`git ls-files` + the untracked new files, `node_modules` symlinked — never `git stash`, whose stack is shared with every worktree on this machine), four named breaks each went red and were reverted: `cli-key-cleared-by-unrelated-write` (the write branch without its "did the body NAME this key" guard) failed both the route test and `P-45`'s byte-identical AC-5.1 assertion; `null-does-not-clear` failed the clear case and the presentation-sibling case; `inForce-mirrors-stored` failed server-side and in the pane; and copy claiming a live change failed AC-5.2. The first draft of break (a) did NOT go red — materializing an empty `cli` over a key that was already present changes nothing — which is itself the lesson: a break has to destroy the value the test guards, not merely touch the same line.
- Gate return 1 of 2, and what it was NOT: the canonical `gates` run came back red on exactly one test of 11 872 — `acceptance-parity.test.ts` `P-06`, which starts four real variant runs and exceeded its own 90 s budget. It is not a test this PR wrote (the diff touches only `P-45` and two inventory counts, and `parity()` registers plain `it()` cases, so every added line runs after P-06 has finished), and nothing this PR changed is on its path. The measurement is the finding: the file takes 42 s alone and took 150 s inside the gate run, a 3.5× slowdown from the run's three overlapping lanes, and P-06 — the most subprocess- and worktree-bound case in the suite — is the first to starve. A scratch copy at the base commit runs P-06 in 1.27 s against head's 1.18 s, so this change did not make it slower. Reported rather than repaired: raising that 90 s budget would have been loosening a guard this PR did not write in order to make this PR's gate green, which is the one move the return counter exists to prevent. The residual risk is stated honestly — the next attempt can starve the same way, because nothing here makes P-06 cheaper.
- Remaining limit: the browser suite and manual QA were not run, so how the new section looks and behaves in a real cockpit is unknown and `needs-qa` carries it; the narrowed-cockpit copy in particular has only been seen through a fixture. The design gate is the same — the tri-state `<select>` departs from the spec's proposed radio buttons because radios cannot express "no stored key", and that judgement is the design reviewer's to confirm, not the author's.
- Review response round 1 (run `4244c646`, `address-review-findings`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed, fixture-tested, browser specs run): the design review's two blockers were both the pane saying something the server could not back — "(from the environment)" with no variable set, and "saved for the next xez you start elsewhere" in a folder whose own start is narrowed again. Neither could be fixed in the pane alone: the response contract had to say WHICH layer decided a value (`…Source`) and WHICH narrowing is in force (`narrowing`). Lesson (real-task verified): when copy states a cause, the cause must be a field the server sends, or the copy is a guess that reads as a fact. The unit tests of the first round pinned the copy and so agreed with it; only a person-shaped read in a real browser with the variable actually unset caught it — the same shape as the 2026-09 "documented a path it had no control for" entry.
- Also observed: a text-slice edit (`s[s.index(a):s.index(b)]`) silently inserted a block at the top of `server.ts` because the end marker occurred earlier in the file than the start; the first test run failed at import and named the line. Search for an end marker AFTER its start (`index(b, start)`), or assert the slice is non-empty, before replacing.
- Review response round 2 (run `c03361ad`, `address-review-findings`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Sonnet 5 — real-task observed, browser specs run): the head's CI failed one browser spec, `settings-appearance.e2e.ts` `expected 7 to be 6`, because it counted the global nav by hand and this PR adds the Terminal section — the same closed-list class round 1 fixed in two other places, missed there because the round-1 search was for the new id in lists, and this spec holds the number, not the ids. Lesson (real-task verified): when a change adds an entry to a registry, grep the browser specs for the registry's OLD COUNT as well as for its ids. The spec runs in a plain node process and cannot import the React registry, so the honest shape is one id list with the total derived from its length; the old assertion was reproduced red (`expected 7 to be 6`) against the new build and the new one green together with `guide-10-settings.e2e.ts` in one invocation.
- Refresh round (run `ba59ca68`, `address-review-findings`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Sonnet 5 — real-task observed, fixture-tested by the focused suites only): the integration attempt was refused honestly because `main` had moved with a real content conflict, and the repair was one bullet in `BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md` § 9 — #801 added a bullet directly after the `config.json` bullet this PR edits, so two adjacent edits collided. Resolving it by keeping both bullets in main's order touched no file of this PR's own diff. Two observations: a refresh task's branch starts at `main`, not at the PR head, so the round has to move that branch (it carried no commits, so nothing was lost) onto the PR head before the recorded-intent merge can name the right parents; and the conflict was in the one file every PR of this feature edits, which is what a shared append-only compatibility inventory costs when several PRs of one feature land near each other.

### 2026-09-21 — Bounded wait for the first team-skills fetch (#777), `bug-fix` step `fix`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, claude (Opus 5) — real-task observed
- Input: issue #777 with its QA evidence (run `5aae0719`, `check6-onboard/onboard-run-final.log` line 8), the leader's brief naming the smaller of two designs, and `packages/xezar/src/skills.ts` / `skills-remote.ts` / `workflows/run.ts`. Deliverable: the bounded first-fetch wait, a two-sentence catalog-state note, `packages/xezar/src/skills-first-catalog.test.ts`, `changelog.d/777.md` and this fragment.
- Observed: **the red proof needed a scratch copy of TWO files, not one.** `skills.ts` at HEAD imports `./skills-remote.ts`, so a copy of the changed file alone would have re-imported the FIXED module and gone green for the wrong reason. Copying both pre-fix files and rewriting their two relative imports to point at each other kept the old pair closed over itself; the copies live at `/tmp/xez777-redproof/` with their sha256 in the phase record, and the run logs are in the task evidence dir.
- Observed: **the fixture's delay had to be in `git`, not in the product.** The bug is a race between a catalog read that returns at once and a clone that lands seconds later, so the fixture shims `git` on `PATH` with a script that sleeps before a `clone` and then execs the real one. No hook was added to the source to make the race testable, and the same shim drives both the fix case (1 s delay, 20 s bound → resolves) and the fall-through case (5 s delay, 100 ms bound → `pending`).
- Observed (test-isolation trap): `xezCacheDir()` is deliberately `XEZ_HOME`-independent in the global layout (`~/.cache/xez`), so pinning `XEZ_HOME` alone would have let a fixture clone land in the developer's own cache. `setActiveStateLayout(projectStateLayout(tmp))` — the pattern `skills-catalog-version.test.ts` already uses — is what actually keeps a team-skills test off the real machine. Worth knowing before writing any new test that touches this module.
- Regression/control: the new file carries one case that is red on the pre-fix pair (`expected undefined to be 'team'`) and five guards that pass both ways — no configured source, an already-local skill, a zero bound (the dry-run path), an unreadable source, and the unchanged historical note text. Focused run: 202 tests green across the six suites that touch skills or `workflows/run.ts`.
- Observed (refresh round, 2026-09-21 02:10, environment not source): after merging `origin/main` the author's `npm run typecheck` went red with three `TS2741 Property 'catalog' is missing` errors in `packages/web/src/api/client.ts`. None of it was this branch: the same three errors reproduced with all three changed sources reverted AND on the exact pre-merge commit. The cause was that this task worktree had **no `node_modules` of its own** — `require.resolve('@qodeca/xezar')` answered the PRIMARY checkout's package, so the cockpit typechecked against the primary's `packages/xezar/dist` built at 06:50 that morning, before the `catalog` field existed on the skills-update response, while the contract it was compared against had it. Running `.xezar/checks/worktree-setup.sh` (the kit's own install) made the worktree self-contained and the same command exited 0. Worth naming because the failure looks exactly like a real contract-parity break in the author's own diff, points at a file the branch never touched, and changes under a developer whenever the primary checkout's build output moves.
- Remaining limit: the fix is fixture-tested, not live-verified against the reported `project-setup` incident — no fresh-project run of the guided setup was performed in this task, so the original incident is reproduced in shape (empty catalog at resolution time) rather than re-run end to end.

- Input: the leader's brief naming the spec, the file list and the four named breaks, plus the read-only spec `.local/xezar/tasks/a0cc9e46-…/467-instance-spec.md` (§ 2, § 2.3–2.4, § 5 PR 1). Deliverable: the resolved setting in five source files, two unit suites, the phase record, a changelog fragment and this fragment.

The usual failure AGENTS.md warns about — a test written after the diagnosis that passes against the bug — had no room to happen, because the assertion existed before the defect could be imagined away.
- Observed: **the scratch-copy red proof is strictly better than `git stash` here, and the kit is right to forbid the stash.** Four proofs ran as `cp $FILE /tmp/x.orig && python3 -c 'patch in place' && npm test -t <name> && cp /tmp/x.orig $FILE`. Peer agents were running in other worktrees throughout; a shared stash stack would have been a live hazard for all of them, and the copy has no such coupling. The `-t "<named break>"` filter also made each proof a one-line answer instead of a suite dump.

The resolution is real but not self-evident from either document alone — the variable is *read* here and *decides* nothing — so the reasoning had to be written down twice, in `DOCS` and in the PR body, to stop a reviewer reading it as a missed obligation. A rule whose exception needs restating in every PR that takes it is a rule worth a named carve-out.
- Observed: the phase record's `CRITERIA` validator wanting criterion IDs *and* an `accepted-by:` line is what forced the honest question "who accepted these, and when" — the answer being the leader's brief quoting the owner's 2026-09-20 default decision, not the spec, which only proposed them. That distinction would have been skipped without the check asking for it.

The control that matters for a PR with no user-visible behaviour is AC-1.1 — with nothing set the resolved value is `workspace`, today's behaviour, and no caller reads the field at all.

This PR's evidence is unit-level and should not be read as evidence the flag works.

### 2026-09-20 — #467 PR 3 (the derived `instance?` liveness field), `feature-implementation` step `implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude (Opus 5) — real-task observed
- Input: the accepted spec at `.local/xezar/tasks/a0cc9e46…/467-instance-spec.md` § 5 (PR 3), read-only in the primary checkout, plus PR 1 and PR 2 already merged on the base and read as diffs first. Deliverable: the contract field, a new server-side checker, the route fill, four red proofs, the unit and route suites, and one folded-in review nit from PR 2.
- Observed: **a five-state enum forced a design decision the file list did not contain.** The contract rule is that a response schema describes EXACTLY what the route sends, and one of the design's five states — `checking` — is only sendable if the probe does NOT block the request. Writing the obvious version (await the probes, answer the truth) would have made `checking` a member the route never sends, i.e. a schema wider than its route. The enum is what chose the architecture: cache plus background refresh, first read says `checking`. Worth saying out loud because the spec's file list reads as if the shape follows the implementation, and here it was the other way round.
- Observed: **`server.ts` still carried a hand-written `ProjectsResponse` interface, and it would have silently swallowed the new field.** AGENTS.md § The HTTP API forbids an API type declared in `server.ts`; what makes it more than a style rule is that the route body was annotated with THAT interface, so `contract-parity` compares the schema against a type nobody updated. Adding `instance` to the contract alone would have compiled, shipped nothing on the wire, and passed parity. Replacing it with a re-export of the contract type was three lines and is the only reason the parity assertion bites. Grepping for `export interface` in `server.ts` before adding a response field is worth one command.
- Observed: **the "never render it as running without a liveness check" promise at `BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md:288` reads as advice and is actually a test.** It was written for the port-memory PR of 0.16.0 and it is the reason the checker has an identity check at all: without one, "something answers on the remembered port" and "that project is running" are the same branch, and a person clicks into another project's cockpit. The promise was findable because the field's own contract comment in `packages/contract` pointed at it — a comment left by an earlier PR naming the gap it was leaving (`the DERIVED instance? field the switcher PR adds`) did more for this PR than any file list.
- Observed: **four red proofs, four scratch copies, and the scratch-copy rule is cheaper than it sounds.** `cp file .local/redproofs/file.orig.ts`, a five-line python edit, one `npm test`, restore, is under a minute per break and never touches the shared stash stack. Break (a) went red in EIGHT cases rather than the one it was named for, which is a signal worth reading rather than trimming: it says the identity check and the no-answer branch share one line, so one edit can only be judged against the whole suite.
- Observed: **the self-review round found a real defect that no criterion asked for.** `lastListen.host` is a hand-editable value that becomes both a URL this server fetches and a URL the cockpit renders; nothing in the spec, the design or the four AC mentions it. The fix is one regex. The generalisable rule: when a stored value crosses from "our own write" to "an argument to `fetch`", the question is what a hand-edited file may contain, not what xezar writes there.
- Kit note: `phase-record.sh check` again named every missing record in one go, and `counters init --none` again had to come first. The self-review counter was consumed correctly but recorded immediately AFTER the edit rather than before it — the record says so. A round that is small and obviously right is exactly the one an author forgets to declare first.
- Limits: the canonical gate list was deliberately not run by this author (the workflow's `gates` step owns it), so nothing here claims a gate result. No packaged-CLI case: unlike PR 2, nothing about the built CLI's argument surface changed. No browser QA, and `skip-qa` is carried on the spec's own instruction that PR 3's QA folds into PR 4's — there is no surface to exercise by hand until the cockpit renders these states.

The three skills now ship the only specification a reporting agent works from, and the day one of them drifts from the schema the reviewer writes the packet the skill showed it, the engine refuses it, and the leader learns there is no verdict at all. `packages/xezar/src/kit-skill-verdict-examples.test.ts` parses each skill's fenced example through the shipped `taskVerdictPacketSchema`, substituting only the skill's own `<…>` placeholders; removing `findingsOmitted` from one example turned two cases red. The authoring lesson: run `bash .xezar/checks/changelog-check.sh --fragments changelog.d` when the fragment is written, not at gate time; it is a sub-second check that the author skipped because the fragment "is only documentation". The reporting lesson: a fragment error cascade counts lines, not causes, so read the FIRST line of that output before the other ten.

### 2026-09-20 — #677 wave 2 slice B1 (the MCP workspace-settings write), `bug-fix` step `investigate`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude (Opus 5) — real-task observed
- Input: the task brief naming the accepted spec `677-spec.md` § "Wave 2 — slice B" item B1, issue #677, and the owner rule of 2026-09-20 ("every key"). (b) "move the action out of `AUDIT_MCP_READS`" and "the audit record … field NAMES only (inherited through the route)" were both wrong about the code: the action was never in that list, and the door that settles a call is the door that records it, so an MCP write gets the MCP door's own row (action id, operation key, payload digest) and no field names at all. Both were corrected in the assertion, not in the code, and reported rather than quietly followed. The lesson for a brief: a spec's `file:line` claims age faster than its intent, and "the mechanics exactly as the spec" is an instruction that has to survive contact with a test.
- Observed: **a record-status flip is never one line.** Flipping five `refuses:` entries to `serves:` in `api-coverage.testkit.ts` was the visible half; the invisible half is that `serves` is legal only for a `covered` inventory record, so it pulled in the five inventory rows, two separate `96 covered` assertions, `get_limits`'s `reads:` entry (a record cannot be both), a REAL new acceptance case (every covered record must map to one), and the published `mcp-parity-coverage-map.md`. The suite made every step of that visible and none of it optional, which is the design working — but a brief that says "flip the records" is describing about a fifth of the work.
- Observed: **`assertIsolated` is the right guard and the wrong guard for a workspace write.** The A/B fixture's isolation helper refuses any dispatch outside the bound project's scope, so it failed the new P-45 the moment the case wrote `PUT /api/v1/workspace/config` — correctly, because the write IS outside project scope by design; that is the whole of the owner's decision. The case now asserts the parts of N-01 that still apply (exactly one dispatch, to that route; project B's recorded state byte-identical; nothing of B or any secret on the leader-visible surface) and says in a comment why the blanket helper does not. Worth knowing before slices B2–B5, which are all workspace writes and will all hit this.

### 2026-09-20 — Reviewer proposals are options, and the cheaper one was also the truer one (#732)

- The brief handed me two reviewer proposals per finding and told me to pick the one I could prove.
  For Minor A that mattered: restricting the fall-back branch to a real refusal would have REMOVED
  a recovery path (a resumed turn that ends silently would simply fail the step), which is a
  behaviour change nobody accepted. Re-wording the note changes one sentence and nothing else.
  Picking the proposal a red proof can cover, rather than the one listed first, is the rule worth
  keeping.
- Minor B's two proposals collapsed into one fix. Bounding the fall-back by the remaining wall
  clock ALSO made `progress.deadlineAt` honest, because `startedAt + effectiveTimeoutMs` is
  exactly when the one budget runs out once the second execution can no longer run past it. The
  second half of the finding needed no code of its own — worth checking for before writing two
  fixes.
- The "never `git stash`" rule has a cheaper substitute than a scratch copy: write the tests
  FIRST and run them against the untouched file. Six assertions red, then the fix, then green,
  with no file ever moved. The scratch copy was only needed for the compile-time proof, where
  what had to be temporarily broken was a different file (`RUNNER_IDS`) from the one under test.
- The compile-time proof needed its own control. Adding a fifth runner id makes the fixed
  `Record<RunnerId, boolean>` maps error — but that alone does not show the OLD shape was
  fail-open. Putting the pre-fix `readonly RunnerId[]` declarations in a scratch file beside them
  for the same `tsc` run did: two errors on the new shape, silence on the old one, in one output.
- A brief's label can name something the tracker does not have. `gh issue create --label docs`
  failed three times ("'docs' not found"); the repository's label is `documentation`. Reading the
  label list before the third retry would have been faster than retrying.

- Observed: **`npm run test:multi-project` has no `pretest` build hook**, unlike `pretest:server-mode`. The CI job must therefore run `npm run build:server` itself; without it the harness reports `BLOCKED` (exit 2) rather than running. The job comment records that.

### 2026-09-20 — A red proof that stayed green, and the break that was the wrong one

- RP-2.4 ("append the leader's prompt inside the numbered list instead of after it") went GREEN on
  the first attempt, and the test was not at fault: the break itself was a no-op. Splicing the
  prompt into `blocks` at index 1 appends it when the brief has one block, which is exactly the
  case the test exercises — so the broken renderer produced byte-identical output. Only a break
  that interleaves the prompt among the numbered LINES made it red. The lesson is the one
  AGENTS.md § Changing a mechanism already states from the other side: a red proof measures the
  break as much as the test, and "the test passed against the break" is two hypotheses, not one.
- Assumption A4 of the spec (is the reviewing task's resolved backend and model readable when the
  tool runs?) held, and reading the resolution path first changed the design rather than confirming
  it: `RunRecord.model` is frequently ABSENT, so comparing raw recorded values would have let the
  independence guard fail open on the commonest case. Resolving both sides through the same
  per-runner default is what makes "neither side named a model" compare equal instead of comparing
  two unknowns.

### 2026-09-20 — A guard that read the task where the product reads the step

- The Major of the #742 review is a shape worth naming, because nothing in the authoring run could
  have caught it. The independence guard resolved "what did the reviewer run on" from
  `RunRecord.runner`, copying the ORDER the cockpit's own `taskRunner` uses — and that order is
  correct for the question the cockpit asks ("what is this task's engine") and wrong for the
  question the guard asks ("what engine produced THIS report"). Both readings are defensible in
  isolation; only "a mix per step" — a headline product feature, stamped per step in
  `workflows/run.ts` and named by the verdict's own `stepId` — decides between them. The lesson is
  the § Changing a mechanism that already works rule in its other direction: before copying a
  resolution order, name the QUESTION the original was answering, not the fields it read.

- Every fixture in the authoring run was single-backend, so the guard passed 16 green tests while
  failing open on the case it exists for. A policy guard's first fixture should be the mixed case,
  not the ordinary one: the ordinary case is the one that cannot distinguish a correct guard from
  an absent one.

- The reviewer's m1 asked for "the engine's one canonical model mapper" so aliases compare equal.
  Reading the engine said half of that is impossible on purpose: `model-identity.ts` normalises
  provider form and whitespace, but `claude-model-catalog.ts` deliberately does NOT surface a
  CLI-resolved id, because pinning xezar to one re-creates the drift it exists to avoid. So the fix
  uses the canonical mapper AND states plainly, in the argument description and in
  BACKWARD_COMPATIBILITY.md, that a tier alias and its pinned id are different names and are not
  collapsed. A guard that is documented as weaker than a reader would assume is worth more than one
  that quietly invents vendor knowledge to look strong.

- The red proofs were taken by copying the fixed source aside, restoring the PR-head file in place,
  running the new tests and restoring by byte-compare — no `git stash` anywhere near a stash stack
  shared with every other worktree on this machine. Nine tests red, including the two rendering
  cases the quoting change rewrites; the fixed file's sha256 was recorded before and matched after.

- The `gates` step then went red on ONE test in a file this candidate never touches:
  `mcp/acceptance-parity.test.ts` P-14 timed out at the fixed 30 s deadline of its `until` helper,
  waiting for two dry-run sessions to reach `waiting` — the FIRST wait of the test, so a startup
  wall clock, not an assertion. That gate run reported 847 s of test time inside 251 s of wall
  clock, which is the signature AGENTS.md § Validation already names: concurrent gate runs starving
  each other. At the identical head the same test is green alone in 5.9 s and the whole file is
  48/48 in 42.8 s. The round was spent proving that rather than repairing anything, and the
  deadline was deliberately NOT widened: a fixed wall clock in a test that boots real sessions is a
  real robustness defect, but editing an unrelated suite's threshold to turn a gate green is the
  move the repair counters exist to prevent. It belongs in its own change, with its own evidence.

- What that costs is a whole gate-return round — one of two — spent on a false red. The cheapest
  reading available to a returning author is the SHAPE of the failure: a timeout in a wait, in a
  file the diff cannot reach, is a load signal; an assertion is a regression. Nothing in the gate
  output says which one it is, so the author re-derives it every time.

- Observed: **the issue's mechanism sentence was wrong, and quoting the code is what caught it.** Issue #679 states that `pruneOldRuns()` "runs on the load path" and cites `store.ts:853`. That line is inside `createRun()` (immediately after `this.runs.set(run.id, run)`), and `grep -rn pruneOldRuns` over the whole checkout returns exactly two hits: the call at :853 and the definition at :1450. `RunStore.open()` — the load path — never prunes, so a store already over a cap keeps every row until the next run is created; the index is trimmed and persisted on creation, not on open. The task brief repeated the same claim, and the acceptance is the code, so the documented rule says "when a run is CREATED, not on `RunStore.open()`" and the discrepancy is reported rather than copied. Everything else in the issue matched the code exactly: the two counts, the deletion set (record, `runs/<id>.ndjson`, `runs/<id>.handoff.md`, `runs/<id>-images/`), the newest-first `listRuns()` sort that makes the evicted records the oldest, and the total absence of a log line.

- Remaining limit: the review's "16 runs dropped from the first 40 minutes" figure is still unverifiable from the current store and this task asserts no number, exactly as #679 says. The AGENTS row carries two sentences and no more by instruction, and a table cell cannot hold a real subsection, so the full rule lives only in BC § 3. The draft PR, its labels and the requested Claude docs review belong to the handoff step, not to this one.

### 2026-09-20 — Review response on PR #753: a door that is strict at one level only, and a write with no read

- Input: the immutable `## Code review` on #753 (REQUEST CHANGES, 3 Major, 3 Minor, 2 Nit, reviewed
  head `e58b1706`), the leader's decisions for each finding, and the B3 diff. Deliverable: M1, M2,
  M3, m1, m3, n1 and n2 fixed on the PR's own branch; m2 (audit rows carry no `fieldNames`) stays
  deferred to B6 by the leader's decision, not by this run's judgement.
- **A write whose route merges shallowly needs a read at the same door.** `PUT /workspace/ui-state`
  replaces an object-valued key whole; the cockpit's own pane copes by reading the bag first. The
  MCP door shipped the write with no read at all, so the documented recipe was not merely
  undocumented — it was impossible. The fix is an action (`get_workspace_ui_state`), not a
  sentence. Worth a habit: when a door inherits a route's merge semantics, check that it also
  inherits whatever READ the route's existing client needs to obey them.
- **"The door decides the key set" has to mean every level the door names.** Reusing the contract's
  tolerant read-side shapes made the top level strict and the nesting tolerant in two different
  directions at once — `z.object` STRIPS an unknown key (erasing the person's whole appearance
  under an `ok`), `z.looseObject` KEEPS one (storing unreviewed keys in a shared file). Same schema
  source, opposite failure. `z.strictObject(<contract shape>.shape)` keeps the contract as the
  source of the SHAPE while the door owns the key SET.
- **A negation is not a negation to GitHub.** The PR body said "this PR does not close #677" and
  `closingIssuesReferences` returned `[#677]`: the parser reads the keyword and the number, never
  the sentence. For a slice of a larger issue the only safe check is the API answer, not a reading
  of the prose.
- **Red proofs on a scratch copy, four of them**, restored and verified by sha256 each time: drop
  the read action, drop the four `strictObject` wrappers, rewrite the route's reason at the door,
  return the raw response instead of the narrowed one. The last one is the break the spec named for
  wave 2 and the PR had re-used the number for something else — running it took two minutes and
  turned an unverified nit into a recorded fact.
- **Kit friction, recorded:** the fix belongs on the PR's own branch (`xez/815fe13f`), which was
  still checked out in the author's peer worktree, so this worktree could neither check it out nor
  push through `worktree-git.sh`, whose refspec is fixed to this run's own branch. The commit went
  through the guard; the push to the PR branch needed a plain explicit refspec from this worktree,
  and a `DELIVERED` record carries the result to readiness. The guard and the review-response role
  disagree here by construction, and the disagreement is worth a kit issue rather than a workaround
  each time.

### 2026-09-20 — review round 1 on PR #765 (the run-store teardown race), `address-review-findings` step `address`, `xezar-review-response`, Claude Opus 5 — real-task observed, fixture-tested
- Input: the reviewer's immutable `## Code review` on PR #765 (Claude Sonnet 5, run `c52f6aed`, REQUEST CHANGES: 1 Major, 3 Minors, head `e08c92f5`) and the leader's brief fixing this round's scope (Major + Minors 2–3; Minor 4 optional). Deliverable: one commit on the PR's own branch, one `## Review response` comment.
- Observed: **"only a vanished directory is a shutdown" and the code that implemented it were two different rules, and the gap was an error-class check.** `existsSync(dataDir)` answers false for `EACCES` on an ANCESTOR as well as for a removed directory, so the branch swallowed a real permission failure that used to be logged on every attempt — and then latched the store closed for the life of the process. The fix is to test the ERROR (`err.code === 'ENOENT'`) and confirm it with a `statSync` that itself fails with `ENOENT`. General lesson for this repo's § Changing a mechanism that already works: **a fail-quiet branch keyed on a state predicate needs the ERROR as well as the state**, because the predicate is false for more reasons than the one being handled.
- Observed: **the latch was the expensive half, and nothing in the test set could see it.** The original two cases pinned the two extremes (directory gone, directory present); the middle — gone and then recreated, which anything that `mkdir -p`s under `.local/xezar` produces, worktree creation included — left every run in memory only, silently, with the index stale for the process's life. Dropping the pending timer and skipping ONE write, instead of latching, makes the same teardown silent and lets a live directory recover on the next save.
- Observed: **a product decision hid inside a test fix, and it had to be written down rather than inherited.** The reviewer's suggested shape was one warning line on the vanish; taken literally that re-opens #631, because the commonest occurrence of the state is a fixture removing its own directory during teardown, where a late `console.*` IS the defect. The decision taken instead — silent on the vanish, one `console.warn` when a later write SUCCEEDS after a gap — puts the line where it can never be late, and it is now in the `RunStore` doc comment instead of in a PR conversation.
- Observed: **"every fixture" in a changelog was one grep away from being false, and the honest count changes what the fix claims.** 17 sites plus two shared fixtures are routed through the helper; 69 files carry the same shape and are not. Routing them is not a review round's work, so the guarantee that was actually shipped is a source scan (`runs/store-fixture-scan.test.ts`) that fails for the NEXT fixture written that way, with the 69 listed by name as a list that only shrinks. A scan with a grandfather list is weaker than routing and stronger than a sentence; saying which of the three you shipped is the part that matters.
- Regression/control: `npm test -- packages/xezar/src/runs packages/xezar/src/mcp/stale-write.test.ts packages/xezar/src/mcp/acceptance-parity.test.ts` → 20 files / 450 tests green twice (45.5 s, 47.4 s); `npm run typecheck` clean. Red proofs on a scratch copy of the whole tree (never `git stash` — the stack is shared by every worktree): BREAK-STORE-SWALLOWS-EACCES → the EACCES guard red (`expected '' to contain 'failed to save runs.json'`); BREAK-STORE-LATCHES → 2 red (`expected true to be false` on `isClosed`, and `ENOENT … runs.json` on the recreate case); BREAK-SCAN-NEW-FIXTURE → the scan red, naming the synthetic fixture and its line.
- Remaining limit: while the data directory is gone the on-disk index is stale and nothing says so until it comes back — deliberate (a line every 300 ms in a state the process cannot fix is worse), but it is a residue an owner should know about. And #631's `EnvironmentTeardownError` is still not reproduced end to end, which is why the PR body now reads `Part of #631` rather than `Closes`.

### 2026-09-20 — #547 review response round 1 on PR #754 (A-PORT-01 could allocate the same ephemeral port twice and pass vacuously), `address-review-findings` step `address`, `xezar-review-response`, pi + DeepSeek V4.1 Flash — real-task observed
- Input: the immutable `## Code review` comment of PR #754 (review run `605b07a3`, GPT-5.6 Sol on Codex) — REQUEST CHANGES with ONE Major at `packages/xezar/test/e2e/server-mode-harness.mjs:180`, reviewed head `88cc8304`, base `681bf457`. Deliverable: two distinct probe ports in `A-PORT-01`, the mandatory red proof on a scratch copy of the port resolver, the review-response comment, the refreshed PR body and this fragment.
- Observed: **the vacuity was in the port ALLOCATION, not in the precedence assertion.** `const envPort = await freePort(); const flagPort = await freePort();` let each probe close its listener before returning, so the OS could hand the same ephemeral port out twice; with `envPort === flagPort` an implementation that lets `XEZ_PORT` outrank `--port` still binds the number the case expects, and both assertions pass. The author round's single scratch break happened to draw two different ports and was read as proof the case was not vacuous — one green sample of a nondeterministic guard is exactly the evidence that cannot show it. The fix holds both probe listeners open until both numbers are known (a bound port is never handed to the other probe) and asserts `envPort !== flagPort` before either CLI launch, so a collision fails loudly instead of passing.
- Red/green proof, both runs of `npm run test:server-mode` at this worktree (scratch `cp` of `packages/xezar/src/cli-settings.ts`, sha256 `b7fbcf1d…`, never `git stash`): with the resolver inverted so `XEZ_PORT` outranks `--port`, `FAIL server-mode: A-PORT-01 an explicit --port must beat XEZ_PORT` (exit 1, 3.237 s); after restoring the file from the copy and confirming the sha matches, `PASS A-PORT-01 … (--port beats XEZ_PORT)` and `PASS server-mode including exact-PID teardown (1777ms)`, 3.230 s for the whole npm script. The harness stays inside its 45 s bound by ~25x.
- Observed (third run to record it, after `d3e1d44e.md` and `7841e6ae.md`): **the skill's `DELIVERED` path and the preflight's `branch.owned-by-run` rule still cannot both hold, and the branch rule is the one that runs.** This run's brief says the fix belongs on the PR's branch; the PR branch `xez/a4bc25a9` is checked out by the author run's worktree and cannot be adopted here, and `worktree-preflight.sh` refuses both a foreign branch and a detached HEAD. The compliant path was the recorded one: put the PR head's content on this task's own branch, commit the fix here, and push `HEAD` to `refs/heads/xez/a4bc25a9` with a plain `git push` (`worktree-git.sh push`'s refspec is fixed to this run's own branch). With own commits present readiness passes on `branch.has-own-commits`, and `DELIVERED` is NOT written — the preflight's own refusal text says a run that changed source on its own branch must write neither record, and a `DELIVERED` file here would make `security-scan.sh` record "this branch carries no change set to scan" over a real one. One of the two texts still needs to say which wins.
- Observed (kit friction, and a silent data-loss one): **`phase-record.sh set COUNTERS "..."` is accepted and destroys the counter history.** `COUNTERS` is one of the eight records readiness lists, and `set` validates only the record NAME (`^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{0,31}$`), so writing the counter narrative with `set` replaced the file that `counters init` and `counter` own — including its `history:` line and the consumed round — and the very next `phase-record.sh check` refused the run with `phase.counters|the COUNTERS record has no "history:" line, so the repair history is unknown`. The file is documented as written by `phase-record.sh counters`, never by hand, but the one command whose name says "write a record" is the one that silently overwrites it. Recovery is `counters init --predecessor` followed by re-consuming the round; a `set COUNTERS` guard, or a COUNTERS entry that is not in the generic record list, would turn that footgun into a refusal.
- Observed (kit friction): the shell guard refuses `cd /Users/marcinobel/Projects/xezar` and also refuses `ls` of ANOTHER run's evidence directory under the primary checkout, while writes to this run's own `task_evidence_dir` are allowed. Reading the predecessor's `COUNTERS` therefore needed the read tool with an absolute path, not a shell command.
- Regression/control: the permanent control is the harness itself — `A-PORT-01` now fails on the inverted resolver with two distinct numbers rather than on an incidental draw. `npm run typecheck` green; no product, contract, schema or migration file changed, so no other suite covers this diff.
- Remaining limit: still a local fixture proxy — no real nginx/ngrok/TLS install was run, and row 12's dry-run-task-through-authenticated-proxy browser case is still not built (conditional on #306). The 45 s harness bound and the CI job's 5-minute timeout are unchanged.
