### 2026-09-13 — #356 and #402 (readiness assumes a run's deliverable lands on its own branch, twice), `bug-fix` step `investigate`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude Code — fixture-tested

- Evidence: run `31d78adb`, base `4f27275`. `infra-tests.sh` §7e/§7f (hashed, both red-then-green) and the `worktree-preflight.sh`/`xezar-review-response.md`/`qa.yaml`/`xezar-qa.md` diffs are the primary evidence.
- Observed: **two issues filed nine days apart were the same bug wearing two branches' clothes.** #356 (independent QA always fails: `branch.owned-by-run` refuses a detached HEAD before the `#322 VERIFICATION` escape hatch is reached) and #402 (`address-review-findings` fails after a correct fix: `branch.has-own-commits` refuses the task's own, honestly-empty branch) both trace to the same assumption — that `--readiness`'s deliverable lives on the run's own `xez/<id>` branch — failing in the two different ways a role can violate it (leave that branch entirely, or leave it empty on purpose). Lesson (recommended): when two bug reports both cite the same predicate family in the same script, check whether they are the same root cause before designing two escape hatches — here one of the two fixes was "don't reach the predicate at all" rather than "widen it again".
- Observed: **"pick the option that changes fewer controls" was decidable by reading one function, not by guessing.** `catalog-check.mjs:291` only requires the full preflight→setup→readiness→gates→evidence→handoff spine when a workflow has a `gates` step; `code-review.yaml`/`design-review.yaml` already prove a gate-less, readiness-less shape is legal. That made "add a `qa` workflow" strictly cheaper to verify than "add a third condition to `branch.owned-by-run`, the one guard every task in the repository depends on" — the issue's own text names that guard's blast radius as the reason it was deliberately left unfixed through the 0.14.0 release.
- Observed: **the regression tests had to prove three different things, not one.** §7e's detached-HEAD assertion is a permanent pin of UNCHANGED behaviour (still correctly refused — the fix is routing that role elsewhere, not loosening this check); its `qa.yaml`/`xezar-qa.md` existence assertions are a real red→green pair (`git stash` on `.xezar/workflows/qa.yaml` and `.xezar/skills/xezar-qa.md` reproduces red); §7f's no-record case is also a permanent guard-both-ways pin, while its well-formed/malformed `DELIVERED` cases are the real red→green pair for #402. Conflating the pin with the regression proof in either section would have hidden which assertion the fix actually has to satisfy.
- Remaining limit: the `gates` step in `address-review-findings.yaml` still runs `repo-gates.sh --fast` against the task's own unchanged tree after a `DELIVERED`-record pass — a no-op confirmation, not a re-gate of the pushed content. The leader chose this over checking the foreign head out (which would reopen `branch.owned-by-run` in the very next step, `--record-gate-evidence`, since that predicate is unconditional there, not scoped to `--readiness`). No real independent-QA or address-review-findings run has exercised either fix yet.
- Update (2026-09-15): independent QA run `04d953b7` completed the post-#416 kit → preflight → review shape without readiness. `DELIVERED` use remains unqualified; `15b1c37d` instead kept commits on its own branch after rebasing (see #347 and #419).
### 2026-09-13 — #369 (a pi tool gated behind `approveTools` stalls a headless run for ever), `bug-fix` step `investigate` (the only writing step), `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run c56f5f00, base `7aa4a02`, branch `xez/c56f5f00`. The A-01 `approveTools` harness leg run twice, both records with a `MANIFEST.sha256` in the primary evidence directory under `investigate/`: red on `7aa4a02` unchanged (`turnEnded: false`, `dialogTimeout: absent`), green on the fix branch after the leg was rewritten to drive a real pi 0.85.1 + pi-mcp-adapter 2.32.1 through `PiRunner` (5 of 5 checks). Node 24.20.0, npm 11.19.0, macOS.
- Observed: **a predecessor's diagnosis (run `ebc394e5`, Codex) was handed over as “verify, not trust”, and verifying it cost one harness run and one adapter read.** Every sentence held: pi hands extensions a `ui` in RPC mode, so the adapter's documented headless fail-closed branch is never taken, the dialog carries no `timeout`, and a hand-written `Deny` settles it. The one thing the predecessor did not say and the fix needed was WHO the RPC client is in each place pi runs — the person's window, xezar's runner, or some other program — because only the middle one is xezar's to answer. Lesson (recommended): a handed-over diagnosis of “nothing answers X” needs the list of parties that could, before the fix scope is right.
- Observed: **the integration harness was the red proof, and it was also the wrong client.** The leg drove pi through the harness's own raw RPC class, so a fix in `PiRunner` could not make it green; the proof had to be rewritten to run the product. A red proof that does not exercise the code under repair is a diagnosis, not a regression test. The same leg on the unfixed revision is kept as the recorded red; the unit tests are the fast red (four cases fail on `7aa4a02`, checked by restoring the three source files from `HEAD`, running, and putting the fix back — no stash).
- Observed: **the first green run failed on the harness's own check text, not the product.** The check looked for `den|refus` in the tool result and the adapter says “declined”; the raw-wire leg had matched `approval_denied` in the frame's error field, which the runner's v1 `tool-result` does not carry. Read the observed value in the failure before touching the product.
- Observed: **one guard test was not a guard.** A case named `GUARD:` bundled the notify-gets-no-response check (true both ways) with the cancel-at-close check (true only with the fix); it went red without the fix, which is what a guard must not do. Split, and the label now says “passes with and without the fix”.
- Observed (problem, environment): **two `repo-gates.sh --fast` attempts on the committed fix failed on nothing but vitest's 5 s default timeout, under a load average of 167 then 208 from ~30 peer vitest processes.** Attempt 0001: 5 timeouts in 4 files; attempt 0002: 24 timeouts in 8 files — a different, larger set, every one “Test timed out in 5000ms”, every one in `src/mcp/` or a provider-probe test the change never touched, while typecheck, `test:unit`, build, `test:package` and the repository checks passed both times. The same 8 files pass 233/233 with `--testTimeout=120000` on the same tree and the same load. Stopped at the second attempt rather than spend the last repair on the machine's weather; the `gates` step re-runs the canonical list. This is the starvation AGENTS.md § Validation describes (the worker cap is per run, not per machine), and [[xezar-slots-vs-throughput]] already says to cap concurrent gate runs at 2 — measured again here.
- Change: `core/pi-dialog.ts` (pure: frame → ask-card question / response, refusal, cancel), `PiRunner` pending-dialog state, `SessionOptions.autonomous` plumbed from both `startSession` sites in `run.ts`, harness leg rewritten, AGENT_PROTOCOL AskUser paragraph and checklist, cockpit pi setup-card copy and its test, three guidance docs, acceptance record, coverage map, changelog.
- Remaining limit: the leader extension answers no dialog and is out of this task's scope, so a headless pi that some other RPC client drives still waits for ever; the docs now say whose job that is. The cockpit ask card's rendering of a pi dialog was not driven in a browser (manual QA: gate `health`, run an interactive pi task that names `xezar_health`, see the card, answer it, see pi's `approval_denied`).

Lesson: reproduce through the product's own spawn path with the environment isolated before naming a cause; a wrapper finding explains one machine.
- Observed: `-c mcp_servers={}` is a no-op in Codex — layers deep-merge, so an empty table removes nothing. Per-server `enabled = false`, with names from the app-server's own `config/read`, works, and a nested object in the thread's `config` is accepted.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine, one Codex version; the `apps` feature is switched off but was not observed with a real ChatGPT connector. Claude Code, OpenCode and pi task runs still load the person's MCP servers (follow-up).

The recorded migration: merge the PR head into this run's branch (which sits on current `main`), fix and gate here, push the guarded ref, then fast-forward `origin/xez/c56f5f00` to the same commit with one explicit named-ref push, recorded in `ownership-migration.md` and the PR comment. No branch was checked out or adopted and no second PR was opened. - Observed: the first runner-level regression fixture was itself wrong (`padEnd` does not shorten a string, so the "61-character" choice was 64); the red log showed the fixture's own assertion failing, not the bug. Re-run the red proof after fixing a fixture, and keep both logs.

### 2026-09-13 — PR #411 round 2 (issue #369), `address-review-findings` step 1 (Address findings), `xezar-review-response`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Kit: `address-review-findings.yaml` and `xezar-review-response` as installed at task start; skill version not declared. Brief named the one Major finding and the exact regression cases.
- Observed: **the round-1 fix folded case away before matching, and the round-1 tests never asked about case.** The label → value map was index-keyed, as round 1 required, but `answerPiDialog` lowercased both sides and took the first hit, so pi choices that differ only by case (`Allow` / `allow`, which `readPiDialog` and the ask schema accept as distinct labels) routed a click on the second to the first. Fix: exact label by index first, exact value second, case-folded match last and only when it identifies one choice; an ambiguous fold cancels. Regression proof: 2 new cases (helper + wire) RED on the reviewed head's `pi-dialog.ts`, 40 of 40 GREEN with the fix (`address/round2-*.log` in the task evidence directory).
- Observed: the PR branch already sat on current `main`, so the reclaimed-owner migration of round 1 was a fast-forward this time: no merge commit, `worktree-git.sh commit` accepted the fix directly, and the one extra named-ref push reaches `xez/c56f5f00`.
- Lesson (recommended): a "normalise then match" step is a second mapping on top of the index-keyed one, and needs its own collision test. Write the case-twin case next to the truncation-twin case the moment matching does any folding.

### 2026-09-13 — PR #403 (issue #374), `address-review-findings` step 1 (Address findings), `xezar-review-response`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: the task evidence directory holds the live probes against a throwaway `codex app-server --listen unix://` (codex-cli 0.154.0, `CODEX_HOME` under `/tmp`, deleted afterwards), `prove-red-round3.log` (18 named breaks, each RED, then GREEN after restore) and the real-listener runs of `mcp-real-clients.test.ts`.
- Observed: **two rounds of green fake-server tests hid three defects that the first real listener showed in seconds.** The `ws` client offered `permessage-deflate` and the real server hung up. Codex starts an MCP server with five environment variables only, so a bridge that needed `CODEX_HOME` never announced anything. A thread subscription held by xezar kept an exited TUI's thread loaded for more than 180 s (it unloads about 57 s after exit without one). Each fake had copied xezar's own assumptions, so it agreed with them.
- Observed: `/usr/bin/script` cannot give a TUI a terminal from an agent's shell (`tcgetattr/ioctl: Operation not supported on socket`). A 30-line Python `pty.fork` host works and records the screen.
- Observed: the real harness also corrected the test. After TUI exit the check expected Codex's own blocker, but the TUI's MCP bridge exits with the TUI, so the owner-session blocker comes first. Both are recoverable and both name `leader_events`.
- Lesson: for an adapter that speaks another program's protocol, build the fixture from a recorded live exchange, and run one real-listener leg before the first review, not after the third. A fake that rejects what the real peer rejects (here: an Upgrade with `sec-websocket-extensions`) is what makes a unit test able to fail.

### 2026-09-13 — PR #416 round 1, `address-review-findings` step 1 (Address findings), `xezar-review-response`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: `.xezar/checks/infra-tests.sh` §7f (520 passed, 0 failed on the fixed head); a throwaway standalone harness reproducing only §7f proved both new negative cases pass with exit 0 against the unfixed check (red), then fail closed against the fix (green); `node .xezar/checks/catalog-check.mjs` OK; `node --test .xezar/checks/xezar-contract.test.mjs` 26/26.
- Observed: **the `DELIVERED` record's own positive fixture WAS the exploit.** #402's fixture proved a well-formed record passes readiness by moving only a LOCAL branch ref with `update-ref` — never pushing anything — and asserting `expect_ok`. That is not an incomplete test, it is the bug encoded as the passing case: `refs/heads/<branch>` and `refs/remotes/origin/<branch>` are both refs the same agent the check exists to hold accountable can write without a single byte reaching the network. Lesson: when a new evidence record's proof-of-fact is "some ref agrees with the record", ask whether the author of the record also controls that ref — if so, the ref proves nothing and the fixture that "passes" it is the vulnerability, not the coverage.
- Observed: **a task whose own review-response mission is "harden the DELIVERED record" has to deliver its own fix through that same, not-yet-hardened mechanism** — this task's own branch (`xez/84f556ef`) differs from the PR's branch (`xez/31d78adb`), same shape as every future run this fix protects. Wrote a genuine `DELIVERED` record for this delivery, checked against the FIXED check itself, so the very next workflow step's readiness call is the first real (non-fixture) consumer of the live-remote-tip verification.
- Observed: **building the throwaway harness to prove red-first nearly caused a real, live `git push` to the actual `github.com/qodeca/xezar` remote.** The harness's first cut extracted `expect_fail`/`expect_ok`/`make_fixture`/`add_worktree` calls without their definitions; every one printed `command not found` and execution fell through to plain `git -C "$root" ...` with `$root` empty. `git -C ""` silently targets the CWD (documented in this very suite's own §"fixture isolation" comment) — CWD was the real scratch worktree, whose `origin` remote is the real GitHub repo, and two `git push` calls actually reached it. Both were rejected by GitHub as non-fast-forward against a real, unrelated historical branch (`xez/939d7d68`, from real PR #401) — pure luck of naming, not a safeguard in the throwaway script. Lesson: a hand-extracted subset of a fixture suite must include the suite's OWN `assert_isolated_fixture_root` guard (or reimplement its non-empty/absolute/inside-scratch/not-the-real-repo checks) before it runs a single `git -C "$root"` — copying the assertions without their common isolation guard reproduces exactly the incident class that guard exists to prevent, in the meta-test written to verify the guard's own sibling fixture.
- Observed: scoped the reviewer's "capture the intended branch/base before the writing step" ask to a documented deferral rather than implementing it — no existing kit or engine mechanism supplies a pre-writing-step, agent-unwritable source for "which branch/base this specific review-response task targets" (the task manifest carries unused `pr`/`repo` fields, but wiring them would need `gh`-based resolution at plain-preflight time, a new network dependency for every kit-check invocation, and this run's own manifest has `pr: null`, so a mandatory version of that check would have failed this very delivery). The live-remote-tip check closes the reviewed exploit (no-push forgery) completely; the deferred half only reduces misattribution of an already-real push to an unrelated already-existing branch, a narrower, non-bypass concern.
- Remaining limit: the live-remote check requires `origin` to be reachable at readiness time — consistent with `worktree-git.sh push`'s existing requirement, not a new dependency, but unverified against a fully offline readiness run.

### 2026-09-12 — take the MCP mutation gate out of the release path (#377 owns its new home), `feature-implementation` step `implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run 71389a7d, base `65f779d`, PR #378. Both Stryker logs and the red-proof harness (hashed) in the primary evidence directory under `implement/`; the deferred scheduled-workflow draft under `implement/deferred-to-377/`.
- Observed: **a leader scope change arrived mid-step, and the cheap thing was that the deferred half was already whole.** The brief asked for the removal AND the replacement schedule; ~35 minutes in, the leader narrowed it to the removal alone because only that half blocked a finished release. Because the deferred half was complete and tested rather than half-written, taking it out was one `git rm` plus a revert of two touched files, and the work survives verbatim in the evidence directory for #377 to start from. Lesson (recommended): when a brief bundles a blocking change with a non-blocking one, build them as separable commits from the start — the removal, then the replacement — so a narrowing costs a deletion rather than an unpick.
- Observed: **a removal has to say where the thing went, and "nowhere yet" is the sentence people leave out.** The first draft of every comment and doc said "it now runs weekly against `main`". After the narrowing that sentence would have been false in the most dangerous direction: a reader would believe the gate was still being measured. The corrected wording says plainly that it runs nowhere automatically, that it is a manual command, and that #377 owns giving it a schedule. Lesson (recommended): when a gate is removed before its replacement lands, the documents must state the GAP, not just the destination. "Moved to X" and "removed, and X is not built yet" read the same to a skimmer and mean opposite things.
- Observed: **the one check worth real minutes was running the real tool, and it stayed worth it after the scope shrank.** Two 29-second Stryker runs over one low-scoring file (`adapters/codex.ts`, 71.13 %) confirmed against real software that `thresholds.break` is still live and still 80 — the base config (`stryker.config.mjs`) exits **1** at the 80 floor with `Final mutation score 71.13 under breaking threshold 80`, while the exit **0** comparison used the deferred shard config (`stryker.shard.config.mjs`), so the pair differs in whatever else that file differs in rather than in that one key alone. The conclusion still holds — the exit-1 log names the 80 floor explicitly, and the base config's non-comment content is byte-identical to `main` — and it does not depend on any of the deferred code.
- Observed: **the removal is invisible to every automated check the repository has, which is exactly why the claim had to be scoped.** `catalog-check.mjs` guards the contract phases and the `onFail.retry` ordering, so it would have caught deleting the `changelog` or `handoff` step; `infra-tests.sh` passed both ways; no test reads the `release` workflow's step list. What it does not guard is a non-contract check step such as `mutation`, which is why that step could be removed without any check noticing. The location claim in the comment is therefore written as "checked in .github/workflows/ and .xezar/workflows/ on this branch" rather than as an unscoped absence.
- Remaining limit: #377 is unstarted work with a measured constraint waiting for it — a GitHub job is hard-killed at 6 hours against a 3 h 40 min run that is slower on a 4-core runner, so a single scheduled job will not finish. That is recorded in `coverage-gaps.md` § 10.8 and in the deferred draft, not left to be rediscovered.
- Update (2026-09-15): #377 PR 1 merged as #433, adding the nightly `mutation.yml` workflow with six jobs; see the 2026-09-15 #377 entry. The issue remains open for its remaining scope.

### 2026-09-12 — #375 (the MCP mutation gate died at its own dry run), `bug-fix` step `investigate`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run e83a36fb, base `2db05bd`, commit `7d9ee41`, PR #376. Both Stryker logs (before and after, hashed) in the primary evidence directory under `investigate/`. The brief handed over the cause and asked for it to be confirmed rather than re-derived, which is what found the third-variant question worth answering.
- Observed: **a release-only gate gets its first real exercise at the most expensive moment there is.** `npm run test:mutation:mcp` was the `release` workflow's first check step, until it was removed the same day — see the entry above. CI never runs it and no PR runs it, and #333 shipped it with an exclusion pinned to one exact test name. #358 added a second guard of the same class a few hours before the 0.14.0 attempt, under a different `describe` name, and the gate died in the dry run before a single mutant ran. Nothing between the two events could have said so. Lesson (recommended): when a check runs only at release time, the thing that protects it is not its own green run — it is a cheap test in the PER-PR suite that pins the check's configuration against the code it filters. Here that test is ~140 lines and runs in 1 ms.
- Observed: **an exclusion written as one exact name is a list of one, and nobody reads it as a list.** The comment in `vitest.mutation.config.ts` explained the CLASS correctly — a guard that reads source as TEXT cannot kill a mutant, and Stryker's `stryNS_9fa48` header says `process.env`, so it fails on the instrumentation — while the pattern beside it matched one spelling. The author of the second guard had no reason to look at a mutation config. Lesson (recommended): when a comment describes a class and the code beside it names one member, the code is wrong. Anchor the filter on the words the class actually shares (`AGENTS.md — ` … `reads and writes no environment variable`) and say in the comment that it matches a class, so the next author does not have to be the one who knows.
- Observed: **"is there a third variant?" is a question with a checkable answer, and answering it produced the regression test.** Scanning the mutation run's own three `include` globs for describes that read a file and forbid `process.` found exactly two. Turning that scan into the test — read the config's real `include` and `testNamePattern`, walk the suite, fail when a guard of the class escapes — was cheaper than writing two hard-coded name assertions and covers the guard nobody has written yet.
- Observed: the two controls earned their place for different reasons. The populated-scan control (≥ 40 files, ≥ 500 names, four named files present) stops "we found no guards" from reading like "no guard escaped" — and it is what proved the red proof was a real break: under the reverted pattern it stayed GREEN while the other two cases failed. The narrowness control (exactly 2 of ~1 100 names may be excluded) pins the other direction, where a pattern that fixed pi by excluding too much would have silently dropped part of the suite from the gate. A live run under the mutation config showed the same thing from outside: `72 passed | 2 skipped`, the two skips being exactly the two guards.
- Observed: **getting "past the dry run" is a different claim from "the gate passes", and the log makes the distinction cheap to state.** The dry run is 45 s; the full run is ~3 h 25 m by Stryker's own estimate. Stopping the mutant phase after ~5 minutes proves the blocker is gone and nothing more, so the PR says so in those words and names the risk it did not settle: `thresholds.break` is 80, the last full run measured 81.39 % on 11 292 mutants, and this revision instruments 11 758 after `ignoreStatic` — +466 against a 1.39-point margin.
- Remaining limit: the new test proves the FILTER covers the class; it cannot prove that no other test in the MCP scope fails under instrumentation for some unrelated reason, and only a dry run answers that. Six other MCP tests read mutated source as text and pass instrumented today, but nothing pins that. The score floor is unverified on this revision — no complete run was made here — so a `break` failure in the release's gate step is a different failure from this one and belongs to #338. The scan is a regex over test sources, so a guard of this class written with a template-literal name, or nested two describes deep with the class words split across levels, would not be found; the populated-scan control would still pass.

### 2026-09-12 — #370 (document pi's `approveTools` limitation for 0.14.0), `docs-maintenance` step `docs`, `xezar-docs-maintenance`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run bdac7068, base `ae6de7f`, commit `35500cf`, issue #370 opened for the PR to close. The brief supplied the measured fact as a table, told the author not to re-derive it but to check it still held, and warned that this release had already shipped three wrong sentences about pi in one changelog paragraph.
- Observed: **"do not re-derive this, but check it still holds" has to cover the NOUNS, not only the outcome.** The brief's outcome table was exactly right and survived every check. Its one supporting sentence was not: `approveTools` is not "the user's own `~/.pi/agent/settings.json` setting" — it is a key in the third-party adapter's own `mcp.json`, either `settings.approveTools` or the per-server key on the `xezar` entry that overrides it (`tool-approval.ts:42-43`, `README.md:408`, pi-mcp-adapter 2.32.1, read from the installed package). The upstream issue #369 carries the same wrong file name. A reader told to leave a setting alone in a file that does not contain it cannot act. Lesson (recommended): when a brief hands over a verified fact, re-check the file paths, key names and versions in it separately from the measured outcome — they travel in the same sentence and are verified by different evidence. One `grep` in the installed package settled it.
- Observed: **a third-party README's "headless" is not this project's headless, and the one function says so.** The adapter documents that "in headless sessions, matching calls fail closed with an `approval_required` result", and D-04 § 3.4 had recorded exactly that, marked "not run". Reading `ensureToolCallApproved` shows the fail-closed branch is reached only when `state.ui` is absent (`tool-approval.ts:170-172`); pi hands its extensions a `ui` in every mode, so the call falls through to `state.ui.select(...)` and opens a dialog with no `timeout`. The measured 121-second kill is the consequence. Lesson (recommended): a documented graceful degradation is a claim about the vendor's own host. Before recording it as this project's behaviour, find the branch that decides it and check which side this project lands on — here it was one function and it reversed the conclusion.
- Observed: **the `docs` stage can only be marked "not run" honestly for so long.** D-04 § 3.4's `approval_required` line was correct bookkeeping when written — it was labelled "not run" and cited its source. Once a run existed it became a false statement in the decision record the setup card's copy declares it follows. Correcting it in place, with the date and the contradicting run, cost three sentences. Lesson (recommended): a "not run" annotation is a debt with a named creditor. When a task produces the run, grep the maintained docs for the claim it settles and correct it in the same commit, rather than adding the new finding beside the old guess.
- Observed: **the design guardian rejected a GitHub issue reference in user-facing copy.** `#369` as link text tripped `no-raw-hex-colors`, which reads a three-digit `#nnn` as a colour. The rule is right — it cannot tell them apart, and the alternative is an exemption. The link text became "issue 369" and a comment in both the source and the test says why, so the next author does not "fix" it back. Lesson (real-task verified): issue references belong in comments and in the `href`, not as visible three-digit hash text in the cockpit.
- Observed: **an issue that says its companion work "is tracked separately" is worth checking.** #369's body said the 0.14.0 guidance "is tracked separately and lands in this release"; no such issue existed. Without one, the standing campaign rule (`Closes #<n>`) pointed at #369 — closing an unfixed bug the owner had deliberately kept out of the release. Opening #370 cost a minute and removed the trap. Lesson (recommended): before a handoff writes `Closes`, confirm the issue it names is the one this PR actually finishes; a documentation PR and the bug it documents are never the same issue.
- Observed: the two-break red proof earned its keep. Break 1 (card line absent) proved the test sees the line; break 2 — keep the line but replace "a pi that is never offered a xezar tool is unaffected" with "every pi task is affected" — proved it sees the OVERSTATEMENT, which is the failure mode this release had already shipped. A test pinned only to break 1 would have passed the wrong sentence. Lesson (recommended): when the risk is a nearly-true sentence rather than a missing one, the second red proof is the one that matters, and its break should be the specific wrong sentence somebody has already written.
- Observed: **an unscoped absence claim is a snapshot, and three of them rotted in one day on this one task.** The brief's ("`approveTools` appears nowhere in xezar's source") was falsified by this task writing the word down. Its replacement ("appears nowhere in tracked `packages/` or `scripts/`", true at `301a172`) was falsified an hour later by merging `origin/main`, because #368 added an acceptance test that both names `extension_ui_request` and SETS `approveTools` on a fixture. Each was true when written and each reached an independent QA as a false user-facing sentence. The repository already required the cure in two places — `mcp-d01-transport-decision.md:37` and `mcp-d05-async-event-contract-decision.md:33` both say absence claims are scoped to what was examined — and #368's own test file wrote it correctly: "Nothing in xezar answers one: `core/pi-runner.ts`, `scripts/pi-leader-extension.ts` and `mcp/adapters/pi.ts` never mention `extension_ui_request`." Lesson (recommended): never write "appears nowhere in <directory>". Name the files you read and the revision you read them at. A directory-scoped claim is invalidated by every future merge into that directory; a file-scoped one can only be invalidated by an edit to a file the sentence names, which is a change its reader can see. A grep that also matches TESTS needs the word "product", or the file list, or it will be falsified by a fixture that sets the key deliberately.
- Remaining limit: nothing checks that the five surfaces carrying this limitation stay consistent with each other — only the card has a test. The docs repeat the measured numbers as prose, so a later re-measurement would have to find all five by grep. The fact itself was not re-measured here: the adapter code path and xezar's silence were re-verified statically (the three xezar files on this path — `core/pi-runner.ts`, `scripts/pi-leader-extension.ts`, `mcp/adapters/pi.ts` — mention neither `approveTools` nor `extension_ui_request`, read at `df828a0`), but no new `xezar serve` run was made, so the 2.8 s / 121 s / 3.0 s figures remain #330 WP5's, cited as theirs.

### 2026-09-12 — #366 (three user-facing errors in the pi leader surface), `bug-fix` step `investigate`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run dc9ce092, base `ae6de7f`, commit `c55b392`, PR #367. Measurement and red-proof logs in the primary evidence directory (`pi-steer/`, `red-proofs/`). The brief handed over the independent QA's own words rather than a paraphrase, and asked for all three to be verified before any of them was changed.
- Observed: **a measured record that does not name the transport it measured turns into a false user-facing claim two documents later.** `mcp-adapter-evidence-pi.md` § *The parked steer* is correct: an idle `steer` over pi's raw stdio RPC is accepted and parked, 0 model requests. It even says "with no xezar in the loop". What it never says is that **nothing in xezar speaks that channel** — the one `PiRpcLink` outside tests is dialled at the leader extension's socket — and over the socket the same command reaches the model at once. The safety comment copied the record, and the CHANGELOG copied the comment, so a limit that belongs to an unused transport shipped as a documented limitation of the feature. Lesson (recommended): when a record measures a behaviour that has more than one transport, the transport belongs in the SENTENCE, not only in the setup paragraph. The reader who does damage is the one who quotes one line into a release note.
- Observed: **the cheapest way to settle "which behaviour is production's" is to drive both channels of ONE process in ONE run.** `pi --mode rpc --extension <ext>` gives you the person's stdio RPC and xezar's socket at the same time, against the same session, in front of the same request-logging endpoint. Three measurements, ~40 s: idle steer over the socket `0 -> 1` model requests carrying the text with `pendingMessageCount` still 0; idle steer over raw RPC 0 requests with `pendingMessageCount` `0 -> 1`; a steer into a running turn landing in the request after it. Two separate runs would have invited "different pi, different state"; one run removes the objection entirely.
- Observed: **the harness that finds a defect can usually disprove the next claim about it, and re-running one costs minutes.** The independent QA of #358 left its probes in its own task evidence directory. Reading them there (read-only, no copy into the repository, no secret) and writing one new probe on top of the same two helpers turned a half-day of pi plumbing into about twenty minutes. Lesson (recommended): before building a runtime harness, `ls` the evidence directory of the task that last measured this code. The kit's cross-task read-only audit rule exists for exactly this, and the saving is the largest single one observed on this campaign.
- Observed: **reading a string is not resolving it, which is how a path that does not exist survived two QA rounds.** `PI_EXTENSION_FIX` named `pi-leader-extension.mjs`; the repository had seven other spellings and all said `.ts`. Every existing test that touched the blocker asserted on `code`, or `toMatch(/leader_events/)` — never on the path. The replacement pulls the path out of the sentence with a regex, requires a real file at it under the package root, and requires its top directory to be in `package.json`'s `files` so `npm pack` really carries it. Lesson (recommended): a user-facing string that contains a **path, a command or a flag** deserves a test that EXECUTES the referent — resolve the path, or assert the flag against `--help` — because a human reviewer reads such a sentence for tone and stops.
- Observed: **a user-facing constant that has no client is a bug waiting for its second client.** Three blockers about an attached leader were written in OpenCode's words while two clients could already be attached. Replacing them with functions over one `Record<McpLeaderSession['client'], …>` table makes a third attachable client a compile error at the table rather than a message that names the wrong server — the same reason `runner-label.ts` is typed `Record<Runner, string>`. Lesson (recommended): when a second implementation of something arrives, grep the FIRST one's product name in user-facing strings on that path; the strings are where a second client is forgotten, because nothing type-checks prose.
- Observed: separating the red proof from the guard is what made the second half of this honest. Restoring `leader-delivery.ts` to the base turned three new pi cases red **and** two of the three OpenCode controls red — the third (`no-owner-session` still says "OpenCode") stayed green, correctly, because that message already named OpenCode. Saying which is which on the PR costs two sentences and tells the next reader exactly how much the suite proves.
- Remaining limit: only the two attachable clients are covered; Claude Code and Codex are pinned only through `NO_LEADER`, and if either ever becomes attachable the `Record` will force the table but nothing will force the TEST. The `fix` fallback inside `#blocker()` is still OpenCode-worded and is correct only because every pi blocker happens to carry its own `fix` — a comment says so, no test pins it. The idle-steer measurement is one host, one pi version (0.85.1); the claim is about what the extension's `sendUserMessage` does when pi is idle, and a pi release that changes that would make the corrected sentence wrong in the other direction.

### 2026-09-12 — #266 (the audit trail promised four origins and wrote one), `docs-maintenance` step `docs`, `xezar-docs-maintenance`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run f48c7be6, base `87d9f0d`, commit `7a3f4d3`, follow-up issue #364. The brief ruled the scope question in advance and asked to be contradicted if the decision record said otherwise.
- Observed: **the decision record's own modal verb settled a scope question in one read, and the same check had produced the OPPOSITE answer on the previous task.** D-06 § 10.1 says in as many words that it "does not upgrade 'should' to 'must'" for N-04's origin field, so wiring three more doors would have hardened something the project consciously left soft; #264, one task earlier, found § 5.2 stating a plain MUST that the code violated and correctly ruled "implement it". Lesson (recommended): when a brief says "declare it" and an issue says "or build it", read the governing record for **should versus must** before anything else. It is one grep, it is quotable in a PR, and it distinguishes scope creep from an unmet requirement without an opinion.
- Observed: **a guard test that scans source text matched the documentation it was written to guard, and the fix for THAT introduced a worse bug that QA caught.** The new `audit-origin-wiring.test.ts` scans production sources for `.channel('<origin>')`; it failed on its first run against a second "door" that was really the prose in `audit-trail.ts`'s own module comment, which QUOTES the call it describes. The first fix stripped block comments with one lazy regex from an opener to the next closer — and **a regex cannot tell a string from a comment**. Route-path strings such as `'/api/*'` and `'/automations/*'` in `server.ts` opened fake comments that ran to the next real closer, deleting ~1 759 of 6 189 lines (~28 %) of that file from the scanned text. `server.ts` is the exact file #364 names as the home of the `ui` door, so a canonical `.channel('ui')` written there passed the guard green. Independent QA of PR #365 measured it and held `needs-qa`. Lesson (real-task verified): a source-scanning test and a well-commented module are on a collision course by construction, but **stripping comments with a regex is not the fix — it moves the blindness somewhere harder to see.** Tracking strings, templates, regex literals and both comment forms in one left-to-right pass is ~90 lines and is correct by construction; a heuristic ("only strip a `/*` that opens a line") narrows the collision without removing it. Prefer a real parser if one is reachable — note that `typescript` 7.x in this repo is the Go port and exposes no `createSourceFile`, so it is not.
- Observed: **the strongest net turned out not to be the call spelling at all.** A second origin can only be created by `trail.channel(origin)` or `new AuditChannel(trail, origin)`, and either way the file must NAME `AuditTrail` or `AuditChannel`. Pinning the set of production files that name either type catches the variable-origin, bound-method, direct-construction and wrong-directory shapes at once, without depending on quote style or formatting. Lesson (recommended): when a guard must prove an ABSENCE, look for the narrowest thing the forbidden change cannot avoid touching — usually an imported type name, not a call site — and pin that; keep the spelling-sensitive scan as the second net that says WHICH value was used.
- Observed: the scan's fail-open worry was closed by the assertion's SHAPE rather than by a separate control — expecting exactly one element means an empty scan fails too. The populated-input case was still worth writing, because it says WHICH failure happened. QA independently proved all three empty-scan modes red (a missing root, a root with no `.ts` file, and a pattern mutated to match nothing), which is the half of the guard that was well built and was never what blocked.
- Observed: **the comment describing the bug re-triggered the bug.** Naming the old pattern in the new module comment put a literal comment-closer inside that comment and ended it early; the file stopped parsing. Lesson: prose that quotes delimiters is code as far as the tokenizer is concerned.
- Observed: **adding a comment to a source file invalidated `file:line` anchors in two maintained documents.** `auditOriginSchema` moved from line 28 to 44 and `auditEntrySchema` from 56 to 72, and `mcp-api.md` cites both. `grep -rn '<file>:[0-9]' docs/` found every citation in one command. A sibling record (`mcp-api-reference-spec.md`) cites the same symbol and was deliberately left alone: it names its own baseline revision and is dated evidence, not a live surface. Lesson (recommended): after any edit to a file other documents anchor into, grep the docs for `<basename>:` and fix only the LIVE surfaces; note the re-checked anchors and their revision instead of implying the whole page was re-read.
- Remaining limit: the guard test proves what production constructs, not what it writes at runtime — a door that builds a channel and never records would still pass. Nothing checks that the four prose surfaces agree with each other; the test names them in its failure message, which is a pointer, not an assertion. A door that writes the NDJSON itself without using `AuditTrail` is invisible to both nets. The guard pins the file, not the line, so the seven documents citing `mcp/index.ts:254` still drift silently. The full blind-spot inventory is written into the test file itself rather than only here, because the reader who needs it is looking at a green tick, not at this document.

### 2026-09-12 — #352 (the MCP coverage floor was red on `main` the day it shipped), `testing-and-verification` step 1, `xezar-testing`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run 692d3584, base `7bcb258`, head `52f48bb`, PR #355. Gate record and the before/after `test:coverage:mcp` logs in the primary evidence directory.
- Observed: **the written exemption this task had to satisfy was itself wrong, and the reason it was wrong is checkable in one file.** `coverage-gaps.md` § 10.6 exempted five branches of `api-reference.ts` because "the only way to reach them is to fake the registry – a test of a tool that does not exist". But the CONTRACT for that input (`packages/contract/src/mcp-api-reference.ts`) keeps `inputSchema` an opaque JSON object on purpose, so a listing with no `properties` is input the contract already allows. Swapping the registry at the module seam `api-reference.ts` already imports through took the file from 77.27 % to 100 % branches, with seven named breaks all red. No source changed.
- Lesson (recommended): an exemption that says "no real input reaches this branch" is a claim about today's PRODUCERS. Check the branch's input CONTRACT before writing it: if the contract is wider than the producers, the branch has callers nobody has written yet, and the empty-or-absent-input case is exactly the one AGENTS.md § *a fail-open helper needs a populated-input guarantee* asks for. "Faking the registry" is a claim about the seam, not about the input, and a seam a shipped module already imports through is not a fake.
- Observed: **the same exemption record held a second stale entry that had already expired by its own terms.** The `codex.ts` exemption said "Ends when #311 merges – re-measure on `main`". #311 had merged as `7bcb258` before this task started, and re-measuring put the file at 96.4 / 82.7 with no new test. A file that no longer needs an exemption kept one, which reads to the next author as a permanently excused file.
- Lesson (recommended): an exemption that names its own end condition should be re-checked by the NEXT task on that scope, not only by the task that owns the follow-up issue. Re-running the scoped command and reading § 10.6's "Ends when" lines cost about a minute here and retired two records.
- Observed: the red-proof harness paid for itself again in its cheapest form — a five-line node script that refuses an anchor matching anything other than exactly once, then `git status --porcelain` on the file before the run and `git checkout --` plus a clean-tree assertion after it. Seven breaks, seven reds, source restored clean every time. Both sides of each two-valued decision were broken separately (`everyCall` to `false` AND to `true`; the description test emitting always AND never), so no test can pass by luck.
- Remaining limit: one task, one machine. `npm run test:coverage:mcp` is green on `main` for the first time, so § 10.1's condition for putting it in CI is met — but nothing enforces that it stays green until that CI step exists, and `bridge.ts` sits at exactly 80.0 % branches, one uncovered branch from red.

### 2026-09-12 — #329 (five places said pi has no home variable; it has one), `bug-fix` step `investigate`, `xezar-bug-investigation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run e74a4465, base `d8c1dc4`, commit `0964596`. The brief named five sites and one instruction: verify the claim before changing anything, and report back if pi turned out NOT to read the variable.
- Observed: **a documented variable is not a verified one, and the difference is one command.** The earlier check that produced the five wrong statements plausibly stopped at "grep the bundle" — `grep -c PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR dist/bundle/cli.js` returns `0`, because pi composes the name at runtime (`ENV_AGENT_DIR = \`${APP_NAME.toUpperCase()}_CODING_AGENT_DIR\``, `dist/config.js:406`). The variable is nowhere in the shipped bundle as a literal, and it works. Lesson (recommended): to answer "does this binary read this variable", RUN it with the variable set and observe the filesystem — a grep for the literal proves nothing about a program that builds its own env-var names.
- Observed: **the cheapest read-proof is a deliberately corrupt config file.** Writing `THIS IS NOT JSON {{{` into `<pinned>/settings.json` made pi print `Warning: Invalid settings file /tmp/pi-probe/home/settings.json` — the pinned path, by name, in the CLI's own words. A write-proof alone (files appearing in the dir) does not establish that the binary READS from there, and a normal run could not distinguish "read the pinned dir and found nothing" from "did not look". Lesson (recommended): pair a write-proof with a break-it-and-see-the-path read-proof; together they cost two commands and settle the question.
- Observed: **flipping the field the issue named would have shipped half a fix, and the other half was one that no reader would look for.** `PROFILE_ENV_VAR.pi` was the site in the brief. Grepping the TYPE (`Record<ProviderId, …>`) found two more: `PROFILE_DIR_MARKERS.pi: []`, which `looksLikeProfileDir` reads instead of the env-var table (a pi profile would have been flagged "doesn't look like a pi folder" forever), and `defaultAgentProfile`'s ternary chain, which ended in `: home.claude` and therefore answered `~/.claude` for the one provider it never named. That last one was already wrong on `main`: `GET /workspace/agent-profiles` lists over `PROVIDER_IDS`, so the shipped pi row has been reporting Claude Code's folder as pi's home. Lesson (recommended): an exhaustive `Record<ProviderId, …>` in place of a ternary chain turns the next provider into a compile error; a chain's final `:` is a silent default for every case nobody wrote.
- Observed: the named-break habit (SDLC.md § The MCP test floor) applied outside the MCP scope cost about ten minutes for four breaks and eight assertions, and the API-level break printed the developer's real `~/.claude` in its failure message — the bug in the wild, quoted by the test. All eight went red; the guard tests that pass both ways (blank-variable fallback, no neighbouring variable reaches pi's slot, OpenCode still recognises nothing) were kept and are labelled as guards in the source.
- Observed: `worktree-git.sh` and the CWD hazard it exists for are real. Writing the handoff file with a `cd` in the same shell call moved the session's primary working directory out of the worktree; the next command had to name the worktree explicitly. The commit and push had already gone through the bound helper, so nothing landed in the wrong tree.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine, one pi version (0.85.1). The `PI_CODING_AGENT_SESSION_DIR` interaction — `PI_` is in `BACKEND_ALLOW_PREFIXES`, so a host that sets it pulls `sessions/` out of a profile dir while credentials stay — is reasoned from pi's `--help` and not observed. The browser-suite boot still does not pin pi; that is deliberate and its requirements are recorded in `docs/testing/agent-browser.md`.
### 2026-09-12 — #264 (a required `operationId` on every mutating MCP tool), `feature-implementation` step `implement`, `xezar-implementation`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run 2667f27f, base `7bcb258`, PR #359. Gate attempt `0001-1789194710332-81815`, sealed on `6771148`.
- Observed: **a per-file coverage floor was already red on `main`, and the obvious reading was that this task broke it.** `npm run test:coverage:mcp` failed on `packages/xezar/src/mcp/api-reference.ts` at 77.27 % branches against the 80 % floor — a file this task never edited. The baseline was measured rather than assumed: the change set was stashed under a unique tag, the same measurement re-run at `7bcb258`, and the result was identical — 22 branches, 17 covered, the **same five locations**. Lesson (recommended): when a per-file gate fails on a file you did not touch, measure the same number at the base commit before you spend a repair budget on it; a v8 branch percentage names no cause, and "my change did this" is a testable claim, not a default.
- Observed, and then DISPROVED by the next task on the same file — read both halves: this entry first claimed those five branches "cannot be reached from any test", because they are defensive fallbacks (`?? {}`, an optional chain, a `!discriminator` path) the real tool registry never produces and `buildMcpApiReference` is the module's only export. **That conclusion was wrong.** #352 (merged as `7a84a1d`, PR #355) swapped the registry at the module seam `api-reference.ts` already imports through and took the file from 77.27 % to **100 %** branches, with seven named breaks all red and no source change — its own entry above says so in full. The error was in the step between the two facts: "the live producers never emit this shape" is true, and "no test can emit it" does not follow from it. The producers are not the input contract, and a seam a shipped module already imports through is not a fake. Lesson (recommended): before writing "untestable", name the SEAM you would have to fake and check whether the module already imports through it; "I could not see how" is a report about the author, and it reads to the next author as a closed question.
- Observed: `git checkout HEAD -- $FILES` with the paths in a plain shell variable failed under zsh — `cp: <all seven paths>: No such file or directory` — exactly the trap the campaign rules name. A zsh array (`files=(...)`, `"${files[@]}"`) worked, and `git status --porcelain` was read before AND after the revert, so the red proof could not come back green on a revert that never happened.
- Observed: a required argument added to six tool schemas broke **167 tests in 16 files**, nearly all of them through five call helpers. A small test-only helper that asks the tool's OWN schema whether an action needs a key (`tools/operation-id.testkit.ts`) turned that into one or two lines per file, and it mints a FRESH key per call, so no two calls in a case silently became one operation — which is the behaviour under test elsewhere. Lesson (recommended): when a schema change reaches hundreds of call sites, put the adaptation in one seam that derives its answer from the schema, never a literal sprinkled through the suite.
- Remaining limit: one task. The floor itself is no longer red — `npm run test:coverage:mcp` is green on `main` since `7a84a1d`, and green on this branch — but nothing CHECKS that it is green on `main` before a branch is judged against it, so the next author meets the same red-on-arrival trap that opened this entry. § 10.1 of `docs/testing/coverage-gaps.md` records the condition for putting it in CI as now met; until that step exists, `bridge.ts` sitting at exactly 80.00 % branches is one uncovered branch from repeating this.

### 2026-09-12 — issue #330 WP5, `testing-and-verification` step 1 (Write and run tests, `xezar-testing`), Claude Code — real-task verified

- Goal: pi's leg of the `#118` real-client acceptance harness, plus a pi column for A-01, A-17, A-18, A-19, A-20 and A-23 on ONE candidate revision. Base `ae6de7f`, branch `xez/c17af4a9`, macOS 26.6.2, Node 24.20.0. pi 0.85.1, pi-mcp-adapter 2.32.1.
- Observed: **another agent's QA probe wrote into this task's live pi session, mid-measurement.** Two prompts marked `QA367-…` arrived as model requests 17 and 18 of a case whose whole claim is that request 1 was the only one. The route in is the pi leader extension's Unix socket: its `0700` directory keeps other **accounts** out and says nothing about other processes of this one, and on this machine every agent runs as one user. The socket lives at `os.tmpdir()/xez-pi-<sessionId>/`, so with `TMPDIR=/tmp` anything globbing `/tmp/xez-pi-*` finds it. **For parallel dogfooding this is a shared-resource hazard in the same class as the stash stack and `pkill -f`:** a task's own measurement can be changed by a peer without either side noticing. The fix that generalises is not "guard the socket" (it is meant to be reachable by you) but *put per-task runtime state in a per-task path, and make a foreign write legible*: each pi now gets a private `TMPDIR` under the task's own scratch, and a model request the run did not cause is reported with its text instead of being counted, because "the leader polled" and "someone else wrote into this pi" had read the same.
- Observed: **running a suite nobody has run since two mechanisms landed is itself a finding.** The #118 harness had not been run since #302 (exclusive ownership) or #264 (a required `operationId` on mutating tool actions). Both had quietly broken an assumption in it: A-01's sequential client legs now met `-32080` from the previous leg's not-yet-exited bridge, so a working Codex read as FAILED; and A-20's `leader_events` ack was refused, so a behaviour that works read as missing. Neither was about the client it accused. The lesson for the acceptance records is the one § Changing a mechanism that already works already states, applied to *test* code: the replacement was correct, and the old scenario lost a guarantee nobody had written down.
- Observed: **the same suite's verdicts had gone stale in the product's favour, and that needs saying as loudly as a regression.** A-17, A-18 and A-23 are recorded FAILED on `ed1492e`, whose base predates #302 by seven hours. On the candidate revision every ownership check passes, for all four clients. A record that only ever gets worse is not being read.
- Observed: a fixture difference can look exactly like a product defect. The Codex A-01 leg failed with `-32080` twice; the same Codex reached a real `xezar serve` and the same A/B world from standalone probes every time, with and without the calls under suspicion. Writing the probes before writing the finding is what stopped "Codex cannot use xezar's MCP server since #302" from being published.
- Observed (problem, PI-06): a xezar tool gated behind pi-mcp-adapter's `approveTools` makes a **headless** pi wait for ever — the dialog is named, but it carries no `timeout` and pi's own `docs/rpc.md` says a dialog blocks until the client answers. Nothing in xezar answers one. Answering `Deny` by hand ends the turn with `approval_denied`, so the named state exists and nothing reaches it. Reported to the owner on #330 with options rather than written up as a limitation, because it fails an acceptance criterion (PI-08).
- Observed: asking "does the tarball carry what the documentation tells people to run" found a one-word defect the unit tests could not see: the pi blocker's only remedy named `scripts/pi-leader-extension.mjs`, and the shipped file is `.ts`. The existing test asserted the text mentioned `leader_events` and nothing about the file. A file name in user-facing advice is a promise about the package's contents and is cheap to check; the new test does.
- Remaining limit: one macOS host. A real model's decision is still unmeasured for every client (OB-5). Whether a xezar pi **task** blocks the same way on a gated tool — `core/pi-runner.ts` answers no dialog either — is wider than #330 and was not measured. pi-mcp-adapter 2.33.0 is still untested.

### 2026-09-11 — #333 / #337 handoff reopened by leader steering (Stryker as a release gate, three survivors, merge `main`), `testing-and-verification` step 2, `xezar-handoff-draft-pr`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Observed: **a record in this task's own PR was wrong, and only the other PR's diff showed it.** `coverage-gaps.md` § 10.6 sequenced `service.ts` and `index.ts` after #311 because "#311 rewrites the code their uncovered branches sit in". Mapping every uncovered line against `gh pr diff 311` (same base, so line numbers agree) found #311 touches **none** of them, and only 15 of `codex.ts`'s 27. The leader had already approved the deferral on that wording. The two files were tested instead (seven breaks, all red), and the record now says what the diff says.
- Lesson (recommended): a deferral that names another PR ("that PR removes this code") is a claim about that PR's diff. Check it against the diff, line by line, before writing it down – a reviewer approves the sentence, not the diff.
- Observed: `worktree-git.sh commit` refused the conflicted `git merge` again (`gitstate.clean`), the second task to meet it (see the 2026-09-11 #330 entry below for the first). `merge-recovery.sh record-intent` must run BEFORE `git merge`; the merge was started over under a recorded intent and committed with `merge-recovery.sh commit`, keeping the same resolution. Both tasks conflicted on this file, because every entry is written at the top of this section.
- Observed: **Stryker's sandbox needed three fixes before its dry run passed**, each found by a failing dry run, not by reading docs: the copy drops the executable bit (the `XEZ_DRY_RUN` mock agent failed with EACCES – fixed with `buildCommand`); the suites read `docs/` and `test/helpers/`, so `ignorePatterns` may leave out only `.local`; and one test that reads `opencode.ts` as text fails on the instrumented copy, so the mutation vitest config leaves out that one test, by name.
- Observed: the real mutant count is 12 530, not the 5 000–8 000 step 1 inferred. With static mutants in, Stryker estimated ~60 hours; `ignoreStatic` (1 238 static mutants) brought the full run to **3 h 40 min and a score of 81.39 %** (11 292 tested, 1 261 of them timeouts). The estimate in the first minutes is noise – it swung between 45 min and 2 h for the first hour; watch the tested count, not the percentage.
- Lesson (recommended): a gate whose threshold must be MEASURED cannot be written and committed in one pass. Wire the run first, start it, and do every other part of the change while it runs – the number is the last edit, not the first.
- Remaining limit: one machine shared with other tasks; the run's time depends on that load, and a loaded machine turns slow tests into timeouts, which Stryker counts as killed – so the score reads slightly HIGH under load, never low.

### 2026-09-11 — #333 (an MCP coverage floor forced together with test quality), `testing-and-verification` step 1, `xezar-testing`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Observed: **the red-first harness caught two of this task's own new tests passing either way, before any reviewer saw them.** Every new test ran against a named source break in an isolated `git archive` copy, never the task worktree (MCP source is frozen by #311). Run 1: 20 of 22 breaks went red; a list stale-part test and a surrogate-pair paging test stayed green. The first was fixed (a same-length rename, so only the digest can see the change); the second pinned a guard no input can reach and was dropped and recorded as dead code. Final: 28 named breaks, all red, every control green. A later case written for a mutation survivor (`task-reads.ts:564`) also passed with its break applied and was dropped the same way.
- Observed: **a sampled mutation run found real gaps in files at 100 % coverage.** 158 regex-level mutants over 33 modules, five per file: 121 killed (76.6 %), 37 survived. `connection-file.ts` read 100 % lines and branches while deleting its `chmod 0600` broke nothing; `resource-ownership.ts` (98 / 92.5) let an unreadable worktree path pass. Six survivors in files this task could touch became seven tests, each shown red; the rest are classified in `docs/testing/coverage-gaps.md` § 10.3. One mutant in `ipc.ts` hung the suite for the full 600-second cap; the last files ran with 120 s.
- Lesson (recommended): prove each new test red against a NAMED break with a scripted list (file, find, replace, test filter), in a throwaway copy, and keep the first run's results. The script is cheap to re-run after every fix, and its first run is the evidence that the check is not decoration.
- Lesson (recommended): a mutation run on this scope is not a per-PR check (see `docs/testing/coverage-gaps.md` § 10.3). Split it by directory across two copies when the time box matters, and stop the first by its saved PID, never by pattern.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine; a regex-level sample, not StrykerJS; a mutant that hangs costs the full per-run timeout.

### 2026-09-11 — #330 WP0 (the pi requirements amendment), `docs-maintenance` step `docs`, `xezar-docs-maintenance`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Observed: **matching the quoted text first, then counting diff hunks, is a cheap proof of "change nothing else".** Each quote was located with `grep -n` before any edit (all seven matched, at unchanged line numbers); after the edits `git diff -U0 <file> | grep '^@@'` showed exactly seven one-line hunks at those lines. ### 2026-09-11 — #330 WP1 (pi against the real MCP bridge), `testing-and-verification` step `tests`, `xezar-testing`, Claude Code — real-task verified

- Evidence: run 95c0a6da. A private node harness (real `xezar serve`, real bridge, pi in RPC mode, scripted endpoint) in the primary evidence directory; the committed output is `docs/features/mcp-server/mcp-adapter-evidence-pi.md`.
- Observed: **four of the first scenario verdicts were wrong, and all four were the harness, not the product.** `human` and `async` matched against text the harness had already shortened for printing; `async` also read its results before the queued messages had arrived; `second` started the client with an empty tool cache, which is not the case it meant to test; `stale` required `isError`, although the real bridge answers a conflict as an ordinary result by design (U-M05) – the requirement was copied from the spike's stub. A fifth mislabelled a probe: "700 s after the last call" ran 440 s after it, because a call in between reset the idle clock the probe was about.
- Lesson (recommended): keep the full observed value in a check and shorten it only where it is printed; when a result is FAILED, read the raw transcript before writing it down; when a probe is about "time since X", compute it from the last X, not from the scenario start. Re-run every affected scenario together on the final harness and say in the record which runs used an earlier revision.
- Observed: a non-final step cannot end its turn to wait for a long run (the `XEZ:DONE` rule). Long scenarios ran as background commands in parallel with the short ones, and the step waited on their completion notices. `Monitor` was refused in this session's permission mode; a background `until` loop did the same job.
- Remaining limit: one task, one machine; the harness stays private, so the lesson is not enforced by any check.

### 2026-09-11 — #295 (last of the #307 flakes), `bug-fix` workflow — real-task verified

- Observed: **CPU load alone did not reproduce the flake; modelling the real neighbour did.** The unmodified `mcp/cli.test.ts` passed 41 of 41 under three parallel full `npm test` runs. Adding a loop that did what `package-cli.test.ts` port-fallback does (boot `serve` on a busy port so it walks up one) made it fail 2 of 53. The cause was the test harness trusting a probed-then-released port. macOS hands out ephemeral ports in order, so the next port is exactly where a walking cockpit lands. `xez serve` booted correctly in every captured failure.

Lesson (recommended): before a long background proof run, write the resume notes and the counts so far to the handoff file, so a lost session still leaves the evidence.
- Regression/control: the new case (a neighbour cockpit on the requested port) was red with the old helper body put back and green with the fix. The three original cases pass both ways; they are guards. After the fix: 120 of 120 under the same load plus the neighbour loop, and 20 of 20 alone.
