### 2026-09-10 — root synchronization, `root-sync`, `xezar-integration`, Claude Code — real-task verified for execution

- Evidence: `9e0070b5` completed kit and sync on 2026-09-10; `8bfcb698` completed the same workflow on 2026-09-14. These recorded runs disprove “root-sync has never run”.
- Limit: `root-sync-preflight.sh` explicitly cannot prove the manager lease. Completed execution is not proof of live lease serialization; no primary-checkout mutation was performed by this sweep.

### 2026-09-10 — concurrent gate runs starving each other (observed during ordinary work, no dedicated task)

- Goal: none — this was observed while several gate runs happened to be in flight at once, and is recorded because `parallel-tasks.md` asks for observed contention to be written down before parallel speedup is claimed.
- Observed: vitest's default worker count is `availableParallelism() - 1`, which is per RUN. Ten concurrent gates on an 18-core machine therefore meant roughly 180 worker processes. The symptom was not a crash and not a failing assertion: unrelated suites timed out at 909s on a single file, and a different 17 files failed on every run. It reads as flakiness, which is why it survived a while.
- Change: a cap in the repository's root `vitest.config.ts` — `min(4, availableParallelism() - 1)` — which holds the fan-out to about 8 of 18 cores at the shipped `maxParallel` of 2, and turns the ten-run worst case into roughly 2.2x oversubscription instead of 10x.
- Regression/control: none, and this is the honest limit of the entry. The cap is reasoned from vitest's own default and the observed symptom; no timed before/after was run. It is also a deliberate NO-OP on CI (a 2-core runner resolves to 1, a 4-core to 3), so a green CI run is not evidence either way, and nothing in any suite pins the cap — removing it is green everywhere. Recorded as a gap in the repository's `docs/testing/coverage-gaps.md`.
- Lesson (recommended): worktrees isolate files, not the host. When scheduling parallel tasks, the shared resource that bites first is CPU, and its symptom is a timeout in a suite that has nothing to do with the change.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine, one observation. Whether four workers per run is the right number on a smaller or much larger host is unmeasured.

### 2026-09-10 — PR #165 / issue #153, INTEGRATION task with a CONFLICT (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: bring a conflicting PR forward, review it and merge it. Two PRs fixed opposite halves of one complaint and touched the same two files; #172 merged first (`38f3333`) and left #165 `CONFLICTING`. Code head `38f3333` (v0.11.2 line), branch `xez/ac977b29`, Node 24 / npm 11, macOS.
- Observed: **the "rebase on a throwaway branch and force-push the PR ref" shape from the #40 entry is not available through the kit.** `worktree-git.sh push` takes no arguments — its refspec is hard-fixed to `refs/heads/$BRANCH:refs/heads/$BRANCH` — so an integrator cannot push a reviewed head to a FOREIGN branch through the guard, only around it. Combined with `worktree-preflight.sh` requiring `xez/<first8>`, an integrator holding a conflicting PR has exactly one fully-guarded route: cherry-pick the PR's commits onto their OWN branch, gate and seal there, open a replacement PR and close the original. The #40 entry's force-push route is still correct git; it is simply outside every kit guard, and it silently forfeits the seal-to-head binding that makes the gate evidence mean anything.
- Observed: **the conflict was in the same six lines both PRs edited, and the correct resolution was in NEITHER side.** #172 had split the one `fetch` out of `http()` into a new `request()` that `submitPrompt()` also calls; #165's `try`/`catch` was written against the old `http()` body. Taking either side loses something — "ours" drops the error naming, "theirs" drops the async submit. Moving the wrap into `request()` keeps both and is strictly better than #165's own diff, because the #168 async route inherits the naming instead of being the one caller left with `fetch failed`. Lesson (recommended): when two PRs refactor and decorate the same function, read what the refactor did to the SHAPE before choosing a side; a merge that only picks is usually the wrong answer.
- Observed: **a conflict resolution needs its own test, and neither parent PR's suite supplies it.** Every #153 test drives the blocking `/message` fallback, which a server with `prompt_async` never reaches, so #165's four tests would all have passed while the modern path stayed opaque. One added case (fail `/prompt_async` at the transport level under `MOCK_OPENCODE_ASYNC_PROMPT=1`) is what pins the intersection.
- Regression/control: two red proofs on the merged tree, one per parent. Removing the `request()` try/catch turned 5 tests red (#165's four plus the new one) and left all 3 of #172's green; forcing `submitPrompt()` to return false turned 3 red (2 of #172's plus the new one) and left #165's four green. Lesson (recommended): after resolving a conflict between two behaviour changes, run the red proof ONCE PER PARENT. A single proof only shows that one half is load-bearing; it is the second that shows the other half was not quietly dropped.
- Observed: the second red proof produced live evidence nobody had — with the async route neutered, the mock's socket destroy rendered as `SocketError (UND_ERR_SOCKET): other side closed`, a real transport failure named by the code under review rather than an injected one. Worth noticing when a red proof's failure OUTPUT answers a question the PR body had recorded as unproven.
- Observed (re-confirms #18/#27): `pgrep -fl repo-gates` while the gate ran matched a peer agent's `claude` process, because the whole skill text is one `--append-system-prompt` argument. The pattern-kill ban in `.xezar/CLAUDE.md` is not theoretical; `pgrep` first is the habit that shows it.
- Gate: `repo-gates.sh --fast` declined its own fast path (node_modules did not match the lockfile in a fresh worktree) and ran `npm ci`, then 7/7 PASS including 420 infra fixtures, on the final head.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine. No UI smoke ran — the change touches no cockpit code. The `HeadersTimeoutError` shape is still injected, never captured from the LAN box, so #153's 300s reading remains an inference. One run of the opencode suite, out of ten, failed a single test whose name was not captured; nine consecutive runs and the full `npm test` gate were green afterwards, so it is recorded as unexplained rather than as a known flake.

### 2026-09-10 — issue #170, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: close the last live browser-suite failure blocking PR #128 (`github.e2e.ts:119`, `Element not found: [data-slot="gh-tabs"] …`). Code head `1b82f90` (post-#169), branch `xez/e7473943`, Node 24.20 / npm 11, macOS. Delivered as PR #173.
- Observed: the cause was a **product** bug, not a test one — the bare `/github` index rendered the whole Issues screen while `GET /api/v1/ui-state` was still in flight, then discarded it via `<Navigate/>`. A user with Pull requests remembered sees that flash too. Fixing the product left `github.e2e.ts` byte-identical, which is the better outcome than a spec patch.
- Lesson (recommended): **when a race will not reproduce because the local machine is too fast, do not add load — delay one response.** A ~90-line throwaway proxy in front of the booted `.local/qa` server, delaying only `GET /api/v1/ui-state` by 800ms, turned a 1-in-5-CI-runs failure into 3/3 deterministic. Two traps, both real: the proxy must live in its **own process**, because `AgentBrowser` drives the CLI with `execFileSync` and a same-process proxy can never answer the page it is serving (the symptom is a 60s `goto` ETIMEDOUT); and the census must be captured by a page-side `MutationObserver` + `requestAnimationFrame` recorder, because polling through the CLI is ~20ms per call and the window is one commit. Clicking during the window is not a substitute — a click that SUCCEEDS cancels the redirect it is trying to catch.
- Evidence: the recorder printed the three states in order, unedited — `/github :: gh-row×3, gh-tabs×1, …` → `/github/prs :: (no gh-* slots at all)` → the PRs screen — which is exactly the census #170's comment named as the discriminator. Before that, 15/15 unmodified rounds showed `gh-tabs×1` on every frame with the click succeeding, so the visibility/hit-test alternative was refuted rather than assumed.
- Observed: **the obvious spelling of the fix was wrong twice, and only a request COUNT caught it.** React Query re-enters `status: 'pending'` whenever an errored, data-less query refetches, and `retryOnMount` fires that refetch every time a new observer appears — so `if (index && uiState.isPending)` and `if (index && uiState.isLoading)` both oscillate against a failing ui-state read: 3939 renders and 1649 requests in two seconds, versus 2 without the guard. Neither spelling failed a normal assertion; the test that caught it asserts `sent.filter(…).length < 5`. Worth generalising: **a render guard on a query's transient STATUS is a loop waiting to happen — gate on the monotonic `dataUpdatedAt`/`errorUpdatedAt` instead**, and pin it with a request count, not just a rendered result.
- Observed: `bootstrap.sh` refused with `existing task asset differs: checks/catalog-check.mjs; reconcile deliberately`, because the primary checkout had uncommitted `.xezar/` edits from a peer task (#167). The committed kit was already present in this worktree through git, so the correct reconciliation was to keep it and skip the bootstrap — copying the primary checkout's dirty state would have imported another task's in-flight work. The refusal is the guard working; do not "fix" it by overwriting.
- Observed: **`main`'s CI has no browser-suite job** (it arrives with #128), so a PR against `main` cannot prove a browser fix on a GitHub runner. The working shape is a scratch branch = `origin/<#128 branch>` + the fix commit, `gh workflow run ci.yml --ref <scratch>`, read, then delete the branch. Build it in a throwaway `git worktree` under `/tmp` and remove it — never check the peer branch out here.
- Observed (independent confirmation of Q4 of #170's comment): the first scratch run was named `xez/e7473943-ci-proof` and failed `repo-git.e2e.ts:164` + `settings-agents.e2e.ts:123` while `github.e2e.ts` passed. Re-pushing **the same commit** as `ci-proof-170` passed all 35 files. The precondition for the branchless failure is therefore *"every ref in the checkout is `xez/*`"* — `packages/xezar/src/server/git.ts:103` filters those names out — and not `fetch-depth`. A xezar task branch is the one branch name under which xezar's own e2e suite cannot see a branch.
- Regression/control: the new unit test is red without the fix (`× opening /github renders no tab at all until ui-state answers`, 76ms) and green with it; the second new test is a CONTROL that passes both ways and exists to pin the oscillation hazard. Full canonical gate 7/7 on the committed head, `npm run test:e2e` = `TEST_E2E_STATUS=passed` four consecutive local times, and `TEST_E2E_STATUS=passed` on a GitHub runner (run 34475970071, 308.63s).
- Remaining limit: the real `github.e2e.ts:119` `Element not found` was never reproduced on this machine, so the #169 `click` diagnostic itself never fired here — the census comes from the recorder instead. A ~60-attempt delay sweep (60–280ms) produced zero click failures because the agent-browser CLI waits briefly for a selector on its own; at delay=120ms the click took ~700ms instead of ~25ms, i.e. it waited out the very window this fix closes. That CLI wait is undocumented in `docs/testing/agent-browser.md` and its budget is unmeasured.

### 2026-09-10 — issue #153 (AC 2), single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: lift the 300s wall off the OpenCode runner's BLOCKING prompt route, the one acceptance criterion #172 and #174 left open. Code head `cf2a85e` (v0.11.2 line), branch `xez/26363063`, Node 24.20 / npm 11, macOS. Delivered as PR #178.
- Observed: **the inference three tasks had carried is now a measurement, and it cost six minutes.** #153, #165 and #172 all recorded "undici's 300s `headersTimeout`" as a reading of the documentation plus timing. A throwaway script — an `http` server that answers after 330s, one `fetch` and one `node:http` request against it in the same process — printed `fetch after 301.0s -> REJECTED … HeadersTimeoutError(UND_ERR_HEADERS_TIMEOUT)` and `node:http after 330.0s -> status 200`. Lesson (recommended): when a claim is "a documented default applies here", the experiment that settles it is usually one file and one wall-clock wait. Run it in the background and write the fix while it runs; the wait is not the cost people assume it is.
- Observed: **`npm ls <pkg>` from a task worktree is not the question you meant to ask.** `require.resolve('undici')` succeeded from this worktree and pointed at `/Users/…/Projects/xezar/node_modules/undici` — the PRIMARY checkout's tree, because a worktree under `.local/xezar/worktrees/` is a child directory of the repo root and Node walks up into it. `npm ls undici` inside the worktree said `(empty)` before `npm ci` and, afterwards, named the only real path: `@qodeca/xezar-web → jsdom → undici@7.28.0`, a browser-test dependency of a private workspace. Both facts were needed to answer "would this be a NEW published runtime dependency?" — and the resolve alone would have answered it wrongly.
- Change: `request()` moved from `fetch` to `node:http`/`node:https` (`agent: false`, no pooled socket), with the request's socket inactivity timeout set to the run's own wall clock and left unset when the run is uncapped. The undici `Agent`/`Dispatcher` route was rejected on the dependency ground above: `@qodeca/xezar` is published, and Node's own http client has no such limit to raise.
- Observed: **swapping a transport moves the test suite's injection point, and that is most of the diff.** Four existing #153 tests injected failures by replacing `globalThis.fetch`; once the blocking route stopped using it they became silent no-ops that passed for the wrong reason. Retargeting them at `http.request` needs a fake `ClientRequest`, which is only viable because `sendRequest` uses exactly three of its members (`on('error')`, `setTimeout`, `end`) — worth keeping a transport helper that small for that reason alone. Production code calls `http.request(…)` off the default import rather than a named import, so the patch is visible at call time; a named `import { request }` from a CJS core module is snapshotted and cannot be patched.
- Regression/control: the new test is red without the fix, with the production message verbatim — `opencode: fetch failed after 0s (POST /session/ses_mock_1/message) — HeadersTimeoutError (UND_ERR_HEADERS_TIMEOUT) …`. The wall is STOOD IN FOR rather than waited on: `fetch` is made to reject with the exact error undici throws at 300s, so a runner still routing the blocking prompt through it fails the way production failed. Two new cancellation tests are controls in the #40 entry's sense — they pass either way — but they are the ones that discharge AC 5, because removing a bound obliges you to name every remaining exit. `MOCK_OPENCODE_NEVER_ANSWER_PROMPT=1` (a request accepted and never answered) is what makes "the run's wall clock" and "`end()`" observable in under two seconds each.
- Lesson (recommended): **when a fix removes a timeout, the PR owes an enumeration of what still ends the thing.** #153's brief asked for it explicitly and it was the most useful paragraph to write: the honest answer here was that an uncapped blocking request has exactly the exits the asynchronous route has had since #172, so the fallback stopped being different rather than becoming riskier. `AGENTS.md`'s "enumerate the transitions out of every state you add or keep" applies to a removed bound as much as to an added state.
- Gate: `repo-gates.sh` 7/7 PASS on the committed head — typecheck, `npm test` (355 files / 6806 tests), `test:unit`, `build` + `check:pack`, `test:package`, and 420 infra fixtures — sealed and verified as currently eligible.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine, and no run against a real slow local model. The 330s probe proves the transport, not the runner end to end. No UI smoke ran (`npm run test:e2e` was not executed and is not claimed); the PR carries `needs-qa` with no exemption, because `SDLC.md` grants `skip-qa` only for docs/dependency/CI-only work. The SSE feed's own possible `bodyTimeout` wall is deliberately untouched and unmeasured — recorded as an open question in a comment at the `consumeEvents()` fetch call and repeated in the PR body, on the project leader's decision, so it does not close with #153.

### 2026-09-10 — issue #177, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: close the `Cockpit browser e2e` failure that #128 had just turned into a merge-blocking required check — `progressive-history.e2e.ts:327`, `expected 365 to be less than or equal to 1`. Code head `d7d5985` (v0.11.2 line), branch `xez/8b5b11b3`, Node 24.20 / npm 11, macOS. Delivered as PR #181.
- Observed: the cause was a **test** defect, and it was none of the three the issue listed. The scroller and its restore were correct at every step; what moved was the **departure** offset, after the spec had already read it. Rows carry `content-visibility: auto` with a `3rem` intrinsic-size placeholder, so a row never yet rendered contributes a guess. Parking mid-transcript renders a fresh band, the guesses are replaced by real — smaller — heights, the transcript shrinks (5672px → 5020px), and because part of that loss is above the viewport the browser's own scroll anchoring slides `scrollTop` down to keep the same content on screen (2386 → 1944). The scroller records that offset and the restore reproduces it. Only the assertion's baseline was stale.
- Lesson (recommended): **`content-visibility: auto` makes a scroll container's height a function of where you have already scrolled, so any offset read back synchronously after a programmatic scroll is provisional.** In this cockpit, reading `scrollTop` straight after writing it is never proof of where the reader ended up: settle first, on both `scrollTop` and `scrollHeight`, the way this spec already settles its arrivals. The same shape applies to every `[data-slot="thread-row"]` surface and to `diff-scroll.ts`.
- Lesson (recommended): **when a browser spec's number will not move, change ONE property of the page and see whether the number moves.** `overflow-anchor: none` on the same scroller made the transcript shrink exactly as before while `scrollTop` did not move at all — which names anchoring as the mover instead of inferring it from a list of candidates. A second probe forcing `content-visibility: visible` removed the shrink itself. Two probes, two variables, no guessing; it took less time than reading the scroller's source did.
- Observed: **the "it passes locally" in the issue was a scheduling accident, and a 20-round local sweep would have confirmed the wrong thing.** The spec is a race between the anchoring adjustment and the next navigation, and this laptop wins it every time — 3/3 green unmodified. Adding one `browser.evaluate` round trip to the spec was enough to lose it. So a local green here is evidence about the machine's speed, not about the assertion.
- Lesson (recommended): **simulate the slow runner rather than sampling for it.** Injecting a fixed 500ms after the park turned a ~1-in-3 GitHub-runner failure into a deterministic one on BOTH machines, which is what made a single-variable red proof possible when the fix and the test are the same helper (the #28 problem). Confirmed on real runners: two dispatches of the unfixed helper plus the 500ms delay failed 2/2, and the same tree with the fix passed 2/2.
- Observed: **`gh workflow run` on N differently-named scratch branches at the SAME commit is how you get N runs in parallel.** `ci.yml`'s `concurrency` group keys on `github.ref`, so repeated dispatches at one ref cancel each other and a 20-run sweep serialises into hours. Distinct branch names at one SHA give the same evidence in one job's wall clock. Delete them afterwards.
- Observed: the discriminating probe recorded in #177's own comment (same commit, non-`xez/*` branch, `workflow_dispatch` → green) had changed **two** variables, not one: a `pull_request` run tests the PR merged with `main`, so `ci-probe-177` and the failing run were not the same tree. It was the plain unfixed `main` dispatch (`ci-177-before-3`) that reproduced `expected 365` exactly, which also settles that the `xez/*` branch name is not involved here.
- Observed (re-confirms #27): `bash .xezar/checks/repo-gates.sh --fast 2>&1 | tail -60` reports the **pipe's** exit code, not the gate's, and a backgrounded run of it recorded `exit code 0` with an empty log. Redirect to a file and read the status, or read the `GATES` line; never take a piped gate's status as the gate's.
- Regression/control: the fix is in `parkCurrentThread`, so the red proof swaps only that helper's body under an identical 500ms delay — old body `expected 442 to be less than or equal to 1`, new body 5/5 green, locally and on GitHub runners. The `never-settled` throw is the control: a park that never stops moving is now a loud failure instead of a silently sampled one, and it passes either way.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine plus `ubuntu-latest` runners. The fix removes the spec's dependence on the ordering rather than on the timing, but the 1px budget still assumes the destination's own arrival settles — a machine slow enough to exhaust `MAX_ARRIVAL_FRAMES` (900) would report "never settled" rather than a wrong offset, and that path has not been observed. Nothing pins the `content-visibility` interaction itself: no unit suite can, because jsdom implements neither `content-visibility` nor scroll anchoring.


### 2026-09-10 — PR #128 / issues #60 + #147, INTEGRATION task (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: review and merge someone else's PR, where the PR itself changes the CI gate that the kit's own merge guard depends on. Reviewer/integrator role. Reviewed head `b768e294` rebased onto `main` at `761c3535` → `50a0fa9d`, merged as `980b542`. Node 24.20 / npm 11.19, macOS, 12 peer worktrees present.
- Lesson (recommended): **after a required CI job is renamed on `main`, check that every open pull request has a FRESH run produced by the intended current workflow and base, and deliberately trigger one where it is absent.** Two separate facts, and only the second one is reassuring. (1) **Merging into `main` starts no run on an already-open pull request.** `ci.yml:3-10` triggers on `pull_request` (targeting `main`), `push` to `main`, and `workflow_dispatch`; with no `types:` given, the default `pull_request` activities are `opened`, `synchronize` and `reopened`. None of those is "the base branch moved". So an untouched pull request keeps reporting the old name indefinitely, and the kit's merge guard, which treats a missing required name as `checks.absent` and absent as a refusal, then refuses it. (2) When a run *does* fire, GitHub resolves `refs/pull/<n>/merge` against the current base, so that run picks up the renamed job — but something still has to fire it. #147's feared window is therefore real; it closes when a fresh run happens, not on a rebase as such.
  **What "fresh" excludes, and the two ways to get it wrong.** Re-running an old execution is NOT a refresh: GitHub re-runs retain the original SHA and ref, so a green re-run of a pre-rename execution proves nothing about which workflow was adopted. A `workflow_dispatch` does produce a new run, but it tests the branch tree rather than the pull request's merge with the base, so its result must be associated to the tree and the checks deliberately, not assumed. **A real head update is the observed successful path** — #174 was rebased and force-pushed and its next run carried the new names. That is a historical remedy, not proof that force-pushing is cheapest or uniquely reliable; `synchronize` fires on any head update, and reopening the pull request is another documented activity. **Do not rewrite history merely to refresh CI** — pick the cheapest action that legitimately moves or reopens the head, and then verify the run rather than assuming it.
  Evidence — pull request #174 had **three** `pull_request` runs across three heads (`ea9328f3` run 34478478391, `6d4f5a92` run 34479169251, `bc3ebb66` run 34479819432). The two that bracket the merge are the contrast; the first is the same picture as the second. **Label the clocks — they are four different things, and conflating them is how the original version of this bullet went wrong:**
  | Clock | `6d4f5a92` (before) | `bc3ebb66` (after) |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | commit *committer* date | 12:51:48Z | 12:58:35Z |
  | push | — | 12:58:43Z *(from the #174 timeline, not read from the runs API here)* |
  | workflow run `created_at` / `run_started_at` | 12:52:06Z | 12:58:46Z |
  | job `started_at` | 12:52:14Z | 12:58:49Z |
  | check runs reported | **`total_count: 1` — `Unit, build, E2E, and package`** (OLD) | **`total_count: 2` — both NEW names** |

  #128 merged as `980b542` at 12:56:19Z, between the two. `bc3ebb66` carries the identical commit subject to `6d4f5a92` with a different parent (`a6444cfd` → `2c9131ed`, itself a descendant of `980b542`) — a rebase and force-push. `ea9328f3`, the earliest head, also reported `total_count: 1` with the old name. Only the post-push run carried the new names. Re-checkable with `gh api repos/qodeca/xezar/commits/<sha>/check-runs` and `gh api repos/qodeca/xezar/actions/runs/<id>`.
- Lesson (recommended), and it is why the bullet above was wrong in its first version: **a head SHA read at query time is not evidence that a branch was untouched.** This entry originally claimed #174 "reported both NEW check names on its own head with nobody touching it". It had in fact been rebased and force-pushed about two and a half minutes after the merge (push 12:58:43Z against a 12:56:19Z merge), and the query happened afterwards. The claim was wrong in the UNSAFE direction — it told the next agent that nothing needs doing after a job rename, which would leave them on a refused merge with no idea why. Compare the SHA against the one that existed at the moment you are reasoning about (`gh run list --branch <b>` shows every head that ever ran), and prefer "N runs, here are their heads and names" over "the head reports X".
- Observed, **as of 2026-09-10 and not a standing property**: the only enforcement of a CI check NAME found here was the kit's own `PROJECT_CHECKS`. `main` carried no branch protection and no rulesets (`GET /repos/…/rules/branches/main` → `[]`, `…/branches/main/protection` → 404; the effective-rules endpoint covers organization rulesets too, so `[]` there is the broader answer, not just the repository's own). Both are settings a maintainer can turn on at any time, so **re-run those two calls rather than citing this line** — the point is the check, not the answer. It is worth making before treating a job rename as dangerous, because it changes the blast radius completely.
- Observed, and filed as **#180**: **`integration-preflight.sh` reads only the FIRST check run of each required name** (`awk -F'\t' -v n="$want" '$1==n{print; exit}'`), and one commit really can carry several runs of one name — a `gh run rerun` makes a new one, and so does the same SHA pushed to a second ref. Commit `46553b91` ended up carrying three `Cockpit browser e2e` runs at once, concluding `in_progress`, `success` and `failure`. The endpoint documents no ordering, so today's newest-first response makes the guard refuse (safe) purely by luck; the opposite order would let it report success on a head whose newest run is red. Pre-existing — the `exit` predates #128 — but #128 doubles the exposure by taking `PROJECT_CHECKS` from one name to two, and the added name is the slow, occasionally-red one people re-run. **Until #180 lands, do not trust the guard's one-line `check <name>: success`; list every run at the head and confirm the newest of each required name is green.** **Superseded:** #180 landed as `e59a905` — per required name the newest run by `started_at` now decides, only once every run of that name has finished, over `?filter=all&per_page=100` with a `total_count` completeness check; anything undecidable is `checks.ambiguous`, which is never a pass. Its own entry is directly below.
- Lesson (recommended): **to prove a CI acceptance criterion that requires a RED run, use a throwaway scratch branch whose name is NOT `xez/*`, dispatch `ci.yml` at it, read the result, delete the branch.** One caveat that cost this task a wasted experiment: **a dispatch tests the branch TREE, while a `pull_request` run tests that branch MERGED with the base** — so a dispatch and a pull-request run on nominally "the same commit" are not the same tree, and a difference between them is not evidence about the branch name. #60's first criterion ("a deliberately broken cockpit assertion covered only by a browser spec fails CI") cannot be shown on the pull request itself without reddening it. The scratch run failed `Cockpit browser e2e` on exactly one spec (`smoke.e2e.ts`, `expected 5 to be 2`, 34/35 still green) while `verify` stayed green — evidence the new job discriminates rather than merely passes. The `xez/*` exclusion is load-bearing and is recorded in the #170 entry above: `packages/xezar/src/server/git.ts:103` filters those names out, so a `workflow_dispatch` checkout whose only branch is `xez/*` fails `repo-git.e2e.ts` and `settings-agents.e2e.ts` for reasons unrelated to the change under test.
- **Remaining hazard, now that the browser suite is a required job:** the `xez/*` trap DOES bite `workflow_dispatch --ref xez/<id8>`, which is the natural way an agent proves a CI fix from its own task branch — observed twice (`xez/e7473943-ci-proof`, `xez/11bc89de-ci-proof`). Dispatch from a renamed scratch branch instead. It appears NOT to bite ordinary pull-request runs: every `pull_request` run in this task was green from `xez/*` branches, including #128's own. **The observation is solid; the mechanism was never measured here.** Note what the helper actually reads before theorising: `packages/xezar/src/server/git.ts:93-103` collects **remote-tracking refs too** (`git branch -r`, `origin/` stripped) and only then applies the `xez/` filter — so being detached does not by itself decide the list, because retained remote refs still count. The #170 investigation records a `pull/128/merge` remote ref present in exactly that situation, and contrasts it with an `xez`-only scratch checkout. **The source-confirmed hazard is the filter itself**, which is real and unchanged; what is missing is a printed list from an actual failing runner. Obtain that before relying on the reassuring half of this bullet. Also unmeasured: whether any other checkout shape reaches the all-`xez/*` state. *(Settled in one respect by the #177 entry above: a clean dispatch of unmodified `main` from a non-`xez` branch reproduced `365`, so the branch name was not involved in that failure.)*
- Observed: **bringing a task worktree to a reviewed head is a fast-forward, not a rewind.** `git merge --ff-only <rebased-sha>` from a branch that is an ancestor does it with no history rewrite, and the sandbox's own safety hook refuses the destructive spelling anyway. Afterwards `repo-gates.sh --fast` recorded a normal attempt for that SHA and `--record-gate-evidence` sealed it. This confirms the #40 entry from the other direction: a rebase invalidates a seal, but re-gating the rebased tree is one command and the seal then keys to the SHA that is actually merged.
- Lesson (recommended): **a shipped CI shell step can be regression-tested locally, without a runner.** Parsing `ci.yml` with `yaml.safe_load` and writing `jobs.ui-e2e.steps[].run` to a file gives the exact shipped bytes; driving it with a fake `npm` on `PATH` reproduced all three `TEST_E2E_STATUS` outcomes and showed the naive `run: npm run test:e2e` going green on `skipped` where the shipped step goes red. That is a real red-without-the-fix proof for a YAML change, which otherwise has no test surface at all — and it is more honest than re-typing the logic into a harness, because a retyped copy can drift from what shipped.
- Regression/control: no source change by this task, so no red-proof applies to it. The controls run were the full canonical gate on the exact merged head (7/7, 420 infra fixtures, first attempt, sealed digest `541a4ad8…`), `catalog-check.mjs` OK, four consecutive green `Cockpit browser e2e` runs on GitHub runners (35/35 spec files and `TEST_E2E_STATUS=passed` each, including on `main` after the merge), the deliberate-break run above, a post-merge tree comparison (`git diff 980b542 50a0fa9d` empty) and a single-parent check on the squash. The guard's refusal path was exercised too: pointed at a commit with no CI run it refuses on both new names with `checks.absent`.
- **Those four green runs cannot establish that the browser suite is stable.** They were enough to justify making the job required — the alternative was a job that silently tested nothing — and they are recorded above as exactly that: the controls for THIS merge. They are not a stability measurement. The very next pull request from this task, `46553b91`, a markdown-only change, went red twice and then green on a third attempt of the same run, and the failure recurred on a second, different commit (`93a39e29`) on a different merge base. Every occurrence was `expected 365 to be less than or equal to 1` at `progressive-history.e2e.ts:327` — never a near-miss spread. Tracked as **#177**.
- **Historical hypothesis, recorded because it was wrong and the way it was wrong is the lesson.** From that repeated, pixel-identical `365` this entry originally concluded "a discrete product race rather than a slow machine". **The observation was sound; the diagnosis was not mine to make from it.** Repeated geometry says the number is geometric rather than jittery — it does not identify which component is at fault, and I named the product without measuring it.
- **Accepted diagnosis — it supersedes the reading above.** The cause was a **test** defect: the departure baseline was stale, while the scroller and its restore were correct throughout. **No product change was involved.** Measured, fixed and recorded by the #177 task, merged as `4ba72c4` (PR #181); its own entry sits directly above this one and is the authority for the mechanism, the probes and the run tables. Not repeated here, so the two cannot drift.
- Three general warnings survive all of it, and they are the reusable part: **a short run of greens on a newly-added, flake-prone gate cannot establish its stability**; **an unchanged re-run that goes green is not evidence of a fix** — it only proves that run landed differently; and, learned the hard way here, **a reproducible number tells you the failure is geometric, not which component produced it.** Say what repeated, and leave the diagnosis to whoever measures it.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine. `SKIP_ALLOWED` was reasoned about but never exercised — no run in this task concluded `skipped`, so the "a check that is not permitted to skip was skipped" refusal stays unobserved here. The 30-minute `ui-e2e` cap was never approached (worst observed job 6 m 41 s), so its behaviour on a genuinely slow or cold-cache runner is unmeasured.

### 2026-09-10 — issue #180, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: `integration-preflight.sh` read only the FIRST check run of a required name, so an older green could answer for a newer red and the merge gate could pass a pull request whose CI had failed. Kit-only; no engine, workflow or `.github/` change. Base `223a284` (v0.11.2 line), branch `xez/f992f5c8`, Node 24.20 / npm 11, macOS.
- Observed: **the bug was live on a real commit, and one `gh api` call settled it before a line was read.** `46553b9151f9e916a219e38534b0085b07608642` carries six check runs, three named `Cockpit browser e2e`, started 13:23:14 (failure), 13:32:07 (success) and 13:39:16 (success) UTC — a `gh run rerun --failed` after the #177 flake. That listing is also what refuted the issue's own suggested fix: "every run of the name must be green" would make that commit permanently unmergeable, so the safest-looking rule is a false refusal with no escape, traded for a false pass. Lesson (recommended): before choosing between two safety rules, fetch the real object each would judge and read what each would say about it.
- Change: per required name the newest run by `started_at` decides, and only once every run of that name has finished (`absent` / `pending` / conclusion / new `ambiguous`). Grouping happens once in the node reader, which emits one resolved line per distinct name — that is what makes the surviving `awk '…{print; exit}'` correct rather than a second copy of the bug.
- Observed: **the same lookup existed twice in the file, and the second copy was DEAD in the fixtures.** The branch-enforced-contexts loop skips any name already in `PROJECT_CHECKS`, and the synthetic ruleset enforced only names that list already covered — so that branch had never executed in 420 fixtures. Fixing it needed a fixture that first adds a context the project list does not name. Lesson (recommended): when a defect has two copies, check whether the suite reaches both before believing a green run means both are covered.
- Observed: **the new rule created a new fail-open path that the old rule did not have — pagination.** The check-runs endpoint pages at 30 and one request returns one page. Under first-match-wins a missing page was at worst a false REFUSAL (`checks.absent`); under newest-wins the newest run of a name can be on page two while an older green stays behind, which is the very bug being fixed, re-entering through the transport. The request now asks `per_page=100` and compares the endpoint's own `total_count` against what arrived. Lesson (recommended): after changing what a check CONCLUDES from a list, re-ask whether it is reading the whole list — a rule that depends on "the newest of all of them" inherits every way the fetch can be partial.
- Observed: the `gh` stub in `infra-tests.sh` keyed its recorded bodies on the full path, so adding `?per_page=100` 404'd every check-runs fixture at once. Stripping the query string in the stub is right (a query selects a representation of one resource, not another resource) and is worth knowing before any future case wants a query parameter.
- Regression/control: 23 new fixtures, each listing the run the OLD code would have picked FIRST — a case whose array order agrees with the fix proves nothing. 18 are red against `origin/main`'s script (`passed 425, failed 18`), 443/443 pass with it. Five are deliberate CONTROLS that pass both ways: two greens, a newest-tie whose runs agree, a single run with no `started_at`, the `?per_page=100` reachability check, and the real `46553b91` three-run shape while a run is in flight — that last one refused correctly before the fix too, by luck of the order the API happened to return, which is exactly what #180 said.
- Observed (adaptation of the #18/#22 red-proof protocol): the tagged-stash proof measures the diff against **HEAD**, not against `main`. After the first commit, stashing the source file proved only the second increment (3 red). The proof that belongs in a PR body is against the base: `git show origin/main:<file> > <file>`, run, restore from a copy. Say which of the two you ran — they answer different questions.
- Observed (independent review, #186 G1): **the fix was reading a set that another rule had already narrowed.** The check-runs endpoint defaults to `filter=latest`, which reduces by COMPLETION time and by check suite — a different rule from the `started_at` ordering the new code claims. On the same commit the default answers 6 runs and `filter=all` answers 10; the four it hides are older browser failures and duplicate `Typecheck…` records from suite `93406922306`. Nothing merged wrongly, because the later runs were green, but the program could not have enforced its own policy. Lesson (recommended): **when a check starts reasoning over ALL of something, audit the request, not just the algorithm.** A default query parameter is a policy decision someone else made for you, and `total_count <= length` proves completeness only *within* whatever filter produced the response.
- Observed (#186 G1, and the sharper half of it): **the fixture that “proved” the request was a tautology.** The stub stripped every query parameter, so it returned the same synthetic history whether or not `filter=all` was asked for — a case asserting “the request reached the resource” cannot fail. The working shape is a stub that RECORDS the raw query and serves a deliberately narrowed `.filtered` body to any request that omits the parameter, plus a case whose two bodies disagree in the one direction that matters (the hidden run newer by `started_at` and red). Lesson (recommended): a fixture for a REQUEST contract has to be able to observe the request; one that only observes the response proves the transport, not the contract.
- Observed (#186 G2): **“newest of 3 runs” is a count, not an identity.** #180 asked the refusal to name which run failed, and grouping had discarded the run id, suite, start and URL on the way to the verdict — leaving an operator to reconstruct the selection by hand from a red gate. Every verdict now carries the deciding run and every refusal the runs that caused it, and the fixture asserts the FAILING id specifically: an assertion that merely finds *an* id would pass on a refusal that pointed at the superseded green run.
- Observed (#186 G3, bounded): grouping keys on the display NAME, while GitHub can pin an expected app per required context. Under newest-wins a newer green from an unrelated app would certify the required provider red. No collision exists on this repository — every run is app 15368 — so the correction is to refuse a group that is not provably one source, and to drop the PR description’s claim that this algorithm is equivalent to GitHub protection. Lesson (recommended): when a review says “your claim of equivalence is unsupported”, the cheap answer is usually to narrow the CLAIM and add a bounded refusal, not to build the general engine.
- Observed (#186, on the previous round’s own reasoning): the claim that reading one page had been only a false-REFUSAL risk before this change was wrong — the old first-match reader could equally accept an older green on page one while a newer red sat elsewhere. Worth recording because it was a comfortable error: it made the earlier change look like it introduced the exposure it was actually inheriting.
- Observed: a fixture can pass for the wrong reason and read as a proof. The tab-in-`conclusion` case was written with a RAW tab, which makes the JSON itself invalid, so `JSON.parse` rejected the body before any field was read and the case was green against the bug. Escaping it (`\\t` through `printf`) is what made it test the field; a one-line assertion now pins that the fixture parses to a conclusion actually containing a tab.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine, and every case is a synthetic body served by a stub — the script was never driven against live GitHub in this task, so `per_page=100` and the `total_count` comparison are fixture-tested, not real-task verified. No commit with more than 30 check runs was available to observe truncation for real. No UI smoke ran and none is claimed; the change touches no cockpit code and no shipped package.

### 2026-09-10 — PR #178 / issue #153: transport QA and integration

- **Live transport evidence:** the review report records the same seven-step prompt sent through the blocking `POST /session/:id/message` route of a real OpenCode 1.18.30 server and local model. `fetch` rejected after **301.0s**, with 4/7 files written, and reported `UND_ERR_HEADERS_TIMEOUT`; `node:http` returned HTTP 200 after **457.1s**, with all seven files written. This extends the author's synthetic 330s probe with a real server/model observation. Lesson: exercise QA at the layer whose behavior changed; browser interaction would not establish this transport guarantee. See [PR #178](https://github.com/qodeca/xezar/pull/178) for posted QA evidence.
- **Check completion, not just harness status:** gate attempt `0002` was reported as exit zero, but lacked `result.json`, the final infrastructure-test tally, and the wrapper's trailing completion output. It was retained as interrupted and excluded from passing evidence; a later complete attempt supplied the result. Lesson: require the completed, validated gate record for the tested revision. A peer process kill was suspected, but its cause was not confirmed.
- **Verify the integrated content:** the report records a completed 7/7 gate at `dfdd09b` and equality between that tested tree and squash merge `223a284`, after the base moved during review. Lesson: compare tested and merged Git trees to establish content identity; separately satisfy the current base-compatibility and CI requirements.
- **Verify acceptance criteria explicitly:** the reviewer reports rerunning the red-without-fix proof (restoring the old runner made the regression fail), then observing 34/34 tests pass with the fix. Error-path guards and all seven cancellation tests were checked by name. A suite total alone does not establish that each acceptance criterion was exercised.
- **Limits:** this was one machine and one model. OpenCode 1.18.30 normally uses `prompt_async`, so the blocking fallback was called directly; no older server was available to exercise that fallback through an ordinary runner session. The SSE feed's possible `bodyTimeout` remains unverified by this QA. These are historical reported results, not checks rerun for this documentation update.

### 2026-09-09 — issue #18, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Observed: `worktree-preflight.sh` (strict and `--readiness`), `worktree-setup.sh` and `repo-gates.sh --fast` ran standalone from a plain agent step. Setup on npm 11 prints "install-scripts not yet covered by allowScripts" warnings (esbuild, fsevents); they do not block.
- Observed: the fast gate recorded `failed` because a parallel issue (#19) broke five project-registration test files unrelated to the change. The attempt stays unsealed. The kit has no "known-unrelated failure" notion, so a blockage by a peer task always shows as an unsealed attempt and the leader decides (here: CI in a plain checkout is the gate). That conservative behaviour is correct; do not add a waiver knob.
- Observed: the red-then-green proof works with the shared stash stack when the stash is tagged, applied by SHA and dropped by tag; a bare `git stash pop` is unsafe with peer worktrees.
- Remaining limit: this entry covers one bugfix task with unit tests only; no UI smoke, no kit workflow steps, no root-sync or integration role was exercised.

### 2026-09-09 — issue #27, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Observed: the full canonical gate (`repo-gates.sh --fast`: typecheck, `npm test`, `test:unit`, build, `test:package`, infra fixtures) passed inside a task worktree on macOS for the first time, on Node 24.20 / npm 11.19, after the two test fixes in #27. Before the fix `test:unit` was the only red command; the cause was in the tests, not in the scratch relocation from #23.
- Observed: `--record-gate-evidence` refuses to seal while the tree has uncommitted changes and asks for a commit plus a gate re-run. A single agent step therefore runs the gate twice: once as a development check, once after the commit for the seal. That is the correct conservative order; do not seal a pre-commit run.
- Observed: the `.xezar/checks/worktree-setup.sh` exit code is lost through a pipe in zsh when read via `PIPESTATUS`; zsh spells it `pipestatus`. Read the `SETUP OK` line, or capture the status without a pipe.
- Lesson (recommended): any test that spawns a child from inside the scratch dir must resolve loaders to an absolute URL and compare real paths in main-module guards, because scratch may sit under a symlinked OS temp dir. Recorded in `docs/testing/local-data.md`. A guard test using an explicit symlink now pins this on every platform, including Linux CI where scratch stays in-repo.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine; Windows was not exercised, and no UI smoke or kit workflow step ran.

### 2026-09-09 — issue #31, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — adapted / fixture-tested

- Goal: add the `release` workflow, `xezar-release-changelog` and `xezar-release-publish` skills and `checks/changelog-check.sh` so one task releases a patch/minor/major with no hand-written changelog brief. Kit-only; engine, `.github/` and `scripts/release.mjs` untouched.
- Observed: `catalog-check.mjs`'s writing-workflow phase contract (#116) requires the step after `evidence` to carry the id `handoff`. The brief asked for `publish`; the step keeps that id (`handoff`) with the publish skill and name, and the YAML says why. A kit brief that names step ids should be checked against that contract before it is issued.
- Observed: a fresh task worktree carries no tags (`git describe --match 'v*'` on `origin/main` answered `v0.11.0` while `origin` holds `v0.11.1`), so the changelog skill fetches `--tags` first. Also observed the version-arithmetic trap the publish skill guards in step c: `main` still held `0.11.0` in `packages/xezar/package.json` while npm served `0.11.1`, because the bot's `release/v0.11.1` bump PR (#32) had not merged; `bump: patch` from that state would rebuild a published version.
- Fixture-tested: 13 synthetic changelog cases in `infra-tests.sh` (two `# Unreleased`, one below a dated heading, fenced and second-level headings ignored, `--require-version` shapes, missing file); a stub check that always exits 0 turned the 7 refusal cases red and left the 6 acceptance cases green, so the acceptance cases are guards, not proofs. The contract test's pinned counts moved to 14 workflows / 16 skills.
- Not verified: no real `release` run. `gh pr merge --match-head-commit`, `gh run watch --exit-status`, the `actions/runs/<id>/approve` call and the `production` environment wait are transcribed from `gh` documentation and docs/publishing.md, not observed from a task. The first real run, ideally `dry-run: true`, is the next trigger; record its evidence here. This gap closed on 2026-09-12 — see the "0.14.0 release" entry below (line 447).
- Remaining limit: the last step must fit every wait inside the agent's own session budget (issue #22 removes only the 30-minute kill for the final step); a Release run held at a required reviewer for hours is an untested duration.

### 2026-09-09 — issue #28, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: kill the intermittent CI failure of `github.test.tsx › the follow-up prompt template menu (#413) › inserting a second template stacks it below the first`. Code head `5e8b30c` (v0.11.2), branch `xez/ba65de24`, Node 24 / npm 11, macOS.
- Observed: the flake was a **test** defect, not a product one, and not the obvious "asserted too early". The helper used *"the option node unmounted"* as proof that the insert had landed. That proxy is false: `insertPromptTemplate` restores focus to the textarea inside a `requestAnimationFrame`, Radix dismisses an open popover when focus lands outside it, and a frame deferred by a loaded runner therefore closes the **next** menu with nothing selected. The helper read the empty menu as success and returned without ever clicking.
- Evidence: an in-suite probe (menu open, `textarea.focus()`) printed `option present: true` → `false`, so the dismissal is real in jsdom rather than inferred. Reverting the helper body alone, with the new regression test kept, reproduced the CI diff byte for byte.
- Lesson (recommended): in this cockpit's suite, **never wait on a popover/menu disappearing as proof that its action ran.** Closing is a side effect a dismiss layer can produce on its own. Wait on the state the action was supposed to change — here the textarea value — and re-drive the interaction (reopen, re-click) until it does. The same shape applies to every Radix `Popover`/`Command` picker in `packages/web`.
- Observed: the "prove the regression test fails without the fix" rule needs an adaptation when the fix and the test live in the same file — `git stash push -- <file>` removes both. Reverting only the fixed helper body while keeping the new test is the honest equivalent, and it is worth saying so in the PR body rather than claiming a stash proof that was not run.
- Regression/control: the new deterministic case queues `requestAnimationFrame` callbacks, releases them with the menu open, asserts the menu really was dismissed, then requires the pick to land. The whole file passed 30/30 consecutive runs (120 tests each) after the fix; the full canonical gate passed on the committed tree.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine and jsdom only. The fix removes the test's dependence on the timing, but it was never reproduced on the actual ubuntu-latest runner, so "the CI flake is gone" stays an inference from the mechanism plus 30 local runs, not an observation of CI under load.

### 2026-09-09 — issue #22, single agent step (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: a per-step `timeout` field in the workflow YAML, so a non-final agent step can raise or drop the 30-minute wall clock that killed two earlier bugfix tasks mid-investigate. Delivered as PR #40 on `main` at `5e8b30c` (v0.11.2), commit `81d7f08`.
- Observed: `worktree-git.sh commit` does NOT stage. It forwards its arguments to `git commit` with no `-a` and no `add`, so an unstaged tree produces a clean-looking "no changes added to commit" and exit 0 without committing. `git add -A` first, then the guard. Worth knowing before a workflow's commit step reports success on an empty commit.
- Observed (confirms the #27 entry): the gate ran twice for one commit — once dirty as a development check, once on `81d7f08` for the seal, which `--record-gate-evidence` then accepted and `--verify-gate-evidence` reported as *currently eligible*. Both full `--fast` runs passed on macOS, Node 24.20 / npm 11.19, including `test:package` and the 419 infra fixtures.
- Observed (confirms the #18 entry): the tagged-stash red-proof protocol held with a peer worktree present. Stashing one source file at a time is the useful shape — reverting `run.ts` alone turned 3 of 4 wiring tests red with a real assertion diff, while reverting `types.ts` produced only import errors. An import error is weaker evidence than a diff; prefer the stash that leaves the test loadable.
- Observed: a test written to pin an UNCHANGED default passes both with and without the fix, by design. That is the control, not a failure of the red-proof; say which of the new tests is which when reporting, or a reader counts it as a gap.
- Lesson (recommended): when adding a field to `workflowStepSchema`, four sites move together — the schema, `packages/contract/src/workflows.ts` (compile-time parity), the `skillStackOf` compact-form guard, and the cockpit's `workflowYaml` serializer. Missing either guard silently drops the field when a workflow round-trips through the builder; the type checker does not catch it, because both are `if` chains over optional keys.
- Remaining limit: no real long-running step was executed with a raised cap — the wiring is proven at the `AgentRunSpec` seam and each runner's own timer is covered by its existing suite, but "a 3h step actually ran 3h" is unobserved. No UI smoke, no kit workflow step, and no `.xezar/` workflow was changed to use the field (a separate task).

### 2026-09-09 — PR #40 / issue #22, INTEGRATION task (`quick-task`, no kit workflow) — real-task verified

- Goal: rebase, independently review and merge someone else's PR. Reviewer/integrator role, not author. Code head `58fd842` (v0.11.2) → merged as `e437c24`. The first recorded dogfooding task whose deliverable is a merge rather than a diff.
- Observed: **the branch to rebase was checked out in a PEER worktree**, so the brief's `git checkout -B xez/8128ea4b origin/xez/8128ea4b` would have been refused by git — and taking that ref would have been operating on another task's checkout, which `.xezar/CLAUDE.md` forbids. The working shape is to rebase on a THROWAWAY local branch inside your own worktree and push the result to the remote ref: `git checkout -B integrate/pr<n> origin/<branch>`, rebase, then `git push --force-with-lease=<branch>:<old-sha> origin HEAD:<branch>`. Use the explicit `--force-with-lease=<ref>:<sha>` form when pushing to a ref other than the current branch's upstream. The bare `--force-with-lease` does NOT compare against the current branch's upstream — per `git-push(1)` it protects each ref being pushed to using the remote-tracking ref we have FOR THAT REF, verified accepted in a scratch repo on git 2.55.0 while the current branch tracked something else entirely. The real trap is the other half of that rule: when we have no remote-tracking ref for the destination, git expects it to be absent and refuses with `stale info` (also reproduced), and a stale local copy fails the same way. That is exactly the state after fetching a branch this worktree has never tracked, which is the normal case here. The explicit form states the SHA you actually reviewed instead of depending on what your refs happen to know, and it is the only form `git-push(1)` does not mark experimental — so it is the safer habit even when the bare form would have worked.
- Observed: **a rebase silently invalidates the sealed gate evidence**, because the seal is keyed by head SHA and every commit gets a new one. Re-running `repo-gates.sh --fast` on the rebased tree is not optional politeness; the old record is evidence for a commit that no longer exists. `--fast` then declined on its own (node_modules did not match the lockfile in a fresh worktree) and ran `npm ci`, which is the guard working as documented.
- Observed: **a force-push DOES fire the `pull_request` CI event, an ordinary push apparently did not.** The pre-rebase tip `afc65f7` had no run at all while its parent `81d7f08` had one; the rebased head got a run within seconds of the push and no manual `gh workflow run` was needed. So "no checks reported" is a GitHub event that did not fire, and the cheap fix is to move the branch again rather than to dispatch a workflow. Never read it as a pass.
- Observed: proving a rebase changed nothing is one command, and it is worth doing before reviewing a line: `diff <(git diff <old>^ <old>) <(git diff <newbase> <new>)` with `^index ` lines stripped. It answers "is this the same change" in a way that reading the diff twice does not.
- Lesson (recommended): when the QA route says "exercise the flow" and the cockpit's own module is the untested part, **run the shipped module directly** (`TMPDIR=/tmp npx --no-install tsx`) instead of writing a test you will not commit. It is honest evidence of the real code, and it is not a test claiming coverage the repo does not have. Two traps: `tsx` fails with `EINVAL … .pipe` when `TMPDIR` is the long xezar tmp path, and a script outside the repo must import by absolute path.
- Lesson (recommended): a live `XEZ_DRY_RUN=1` cockpit is the fastest QA instrument in this repo. Booting one via `npm run test:e2e` leaves the instance up (`.local/qa/test-env.json` carries the port), so the browser suite and a hand-driven API pass can share one boot. Drive the same endpoints the UI drives — parse, save, reload, `POST /runs` — and every refusal path is one `curl` each. Always `sh scripts/test-env-down.sh` afterwards, and delete anything the save route wrote into `.xezar/workflows/`.
- Observed: `gh pr review --approve` is refused with `Can not approve your own pull request` whenever the authoring task and the reviewing task share the GitHub account, which is always in this setup. The review still has to exist as a durable artifact, so post it with `gh pr comment` and say in the text why it is a comment. `integration-preflight.sh` already understands this — it reported `policy for main requires 0 approving reviews` and named the authority record as the review that matters.
- Regression/control: no source change, so no red-proof applies. The controls run were the full canonical gate on the exact merged head (7/7 pass, 419 infra fixtures), GitHub CI on the same head, `npm run test:e2e` = `TEST_E2E_STATUS=passed` with a real Chrome, and a post-merge tree comparison (`git diff <squash-commit> <reviewed-head>` empty) proving the merged content is what was reviewed.
- Remaining limit: one macOS machine. The Chrome profile was locked by another local session, so the workflow canvas was never clicked through visually — the cockpit code was exercised as a module and over HTTP instead. `--force-with-lease` was never observed being REFUSED, so the "someone else moved the branch" stop path is untested.

- Goal: design-debt batch B5 (thread, composer and launch menus): 17 allowlist keys converted, 44 px phone targets, no-hover reveals, motion guards, the shared clipboard helper and the danger confirm, with a unit and a browser test.

- Observed: the red proof by restoring the base revision of the batch's source files (not the tests) turned 15 of 18 unit tests and the browser matrix red; three unit tests and two browser tests pass both ways and are guards (the old clipboard path was already honest; the delivery guard needed two locks removed before it went red). Report which is which.
- Observed: the browser matrix found three defects the unit contracts could not: the thread footer's PR link (28 px wide), the attachment thumb at compact densities (`size-12` scales to 36 px), and the jump pill, whose new 44 px height made its fixed `-top-12` offset overlap the dock at "Compact for real". It also found third-party Markdown code-block buttons outside the batch (recorded as G-36, reported by the spec rather than excluded silently).
